CQE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Whether the system itself creates barriers that prevent operators from succeeding): Deming taught that the vast majority of quality problems stem from the system, not the worker, so leadership must first examine whether the system itself blocks success before assuming resistance reflects a bad attitude. Option A is wrong because a large bonus does not address whether the procedure itself is workable and may simply mask a system-level flaw. Option B is wrong because jumping to discipline ignores Deming's core teaching that most failures originate in management-controlled systems, not individual will. Option D is wrong because tightening attendance does not address whether the new procedure is actually feasible for operators to follow.
  2. The correct answer is B (Deriving measurable quality objectives directly from the organization's stated strategic goals): Quality objectives should cascade directly from the organization's strategic goals so that daily quality work visibly supports the business direction leadership has set. Option A is wrong because copying a competitor's objectives ignores the unique strategy, resources, and customer base of this organization. Option C is wrong because basing objectives on last year's nonconformance count instead of forward strategy is reactive and disconnected from where the business is heading. Option D is wrong because letting departments set objectives in isolation risks misalignment and duplicated or conflicting effort across the business.
  3. The correct answer is D (Functional benchmarking, chosen because it can reveal breakthrough process ideas from unrelated fields): Comparing performance against best-in-class organizations outside one's own industry is functional (or generic) benchmarking, and it is valuable precisely because breakthrough process ideas often come from unrelated fields facing a similar underlying process. Option A is wrong because competitive benchmarking is limited to direct competitors and rarely yields breakthrough ideas since rivals often share similar constraints. Option B is wrong because internal benchmarking only compares units within the same firm and cannot capture outside best practice. Option C is wrong because collaborative benchmarking describes a formal data-sharing arrangement, which is a different mechanism than simply studying an outside best-in-class performer.
  4. The correct answer is A (Their input is sought before the decision, but another named role owns the final sign-off): In a RACI chart, 'Consulted' means the person's expertise or opinion is sought as input before a decision, but the 'Responsible' or 'Accountable' role actually owns and signs off on the outcome. Option B is wrong because being 'Responsible' would require the engineer to personally complete and sign the review, which is not what 'Consulted' means. Option C is wrong because being merely 'Informed' would mean receiving the outcome after the fact, which is a distinct, lesser role than 'Consulted'. Option D is wrong because 'Consulted' does not grant veto power; it only guarantees that input is sought before the accountable party decides.
  5. The correct answer is B (The two tasks are scheduled to run concurrently rather than strictly one after the other): Overlapping bars on a Gantt chart show that two tasks are scheduled to occur concurrently rather than in strict sequence, which is how the schedule was actually planned. Option A is wrong because overlap on the schedule does not indicate cancellation of either task; both remain active concurrently. Option C is wrong because if the pilot run truly could not start until qualification finished, the bars would not overlap at all on the chart. Option D is wrong because overlapping time bars reflect a scheduling relationship, not a claim about shared resource assignment.
  6. The correct answer is B (Real-time data entry and retrieval so trends are visible before the next shift begins): A quality information system only supports timely corrective action if data is entered and available in real time, letting the next shift see and act on emerging trends before they worsen. Option A is wrong because popularity among other companies says nothing about whether the system fits this plant's actual data and timing needs. Option C is wrong because cost matters, but selecting on price alone while ignoring timeliness of data undermines the system's core purpose. Option D is wrong because compatibility with unrelated design software has no bearing on the system's ability to support quality decisions.
  7. The correct answer is D (Disclose the falsification through appropriate channels because public safety outweighs personal loyalty): The ASQ Code of Ethics requires holding safety and the public interest paramount, so knowingly discovered falsification of data must be disclosed through appropriate channels rather than concealed. Option A is wrong because assuming the batch is 'probably fine' ignores that the data was deliberately falsified and cannot be trusted to judge actual conformance. Option B is wrong because waiting for a future audit to mention it informally delays action on a known ethical violation with potential safety implications. Option C is wrong because silently correcting the record without disclosure fails to address the underlying integrity violation or hold anyone accountable.
  8. The correct answer is D (Establishing clear ground rules and shared goals so members understand expectations from the outset): Establishing clear ground rules and shared goals during a team's formation stage gives members a common understanding of expectations, which research on team development shows strongly supports long-term effectiveness. Option A is wrong because assigning the hardest tasks to newcomers as a test undermines trust and does not build the foundation a new team needs. Option B is wrong because avoiding conflict discussion for appearance's sake only delays inevitable issues and weakens genuine team cohesion. Option C is wrong because providing no leadership input during the team's formation stage leaves a new team without the direction it typically needs early on.
  9. The correct answer is C (Nominal group technique, since members write ideas silently before any open discussion occurs): Nominal group technique has members generate ideas silently in writing before any discussion, which prevents a dominant voice from anchoring or suppressing quieter members' contributions. Option A is wrong because traditional open brainstorming allows dominant personalities to steer the conversation, which is exactly the problem the team is facing. Option B is wrong because a round-robin with no silent step built in first still risks anchoring since members hear others' ideas before forming their own. Option D is wrong because an unstructured interview that only questions the dominant voice would worsen, not solve, the participation imbalance.
  10. The correct answer is B (Force-field analysis, which maps driving and restraining forces to identify where effort should focus): Force-field analysis is specifically designed to map the forces driving a change against the forces restraining it, helping the team see where to focus effort to overcome resistance. Option A is wrong because a Pareto chart ranks defect frequency and is not designed to separate driving from restraining forces around a change. Option C is wrong because a control chart tracks a process characteristic over time against statistical limits, not qualitative change-related forces. Option D is wrong because a scatter diagram reveals correlation between two numeric variables, not the social or organizational forces behind resistance.
  11. The correct answer is A (Presenting objective evidence and framing the findings around shared improvement goals): Presenting objective evidence while framing findings around shared improvement goals keeps a defensive manager engaged in solving the problem rather than reacting to a perceived personal attack. Option B is wrong because an email-only report with no verbal discussion removes the chance to manage tone and address reactions in real time. Option C is wrong because softening findings so much that severity is lost fails the manager's need for accurate information to act on. Option D is wrong because publicly delivering criticism in a full staff meeting is likely to increase defensiveness rather than reduce it.
  12. The correct answer is C (Contact the customer to gather specific usage conditions before attempting root cause work): Gathering specific usage conditions first is essential because an intermittent, condition-dependent failure cannot be properly root-caused without understanding how and where the product was actually used. Option A is wrong because issuing a refund without an investigation skips understanding the failure mode and may not prevent recurrence for other customers. Option B is wrong because rejecting a complaint for lacking preferred detail damages the customer relationship and ignores a real reported issue. Option D is wrong because waiting for a second complaint delays action and risks the same failure affecting more customers in the meantime.
  13. The correct answer is C (Conducting an on-site assessment of the supplier's process capability and quality system): Supplier qualification, which happens before placing production orders, typically involves an on-site assessment of the potential supplier's process capability and quality system to confirm they can meet requirements. Option A is wrong because granting skip-lot privileges is a benefit typically extended to an already-certified supplier with a proven track record, not a new one being qualified. Option B is wrong because removing incoming inspection before any track record exists ignores that the supplier has not yet demonstrated real performance. Option D is wrong because reviewing years of shipment trend data describes ongoing certification monitoring, not the initial pre-order qualification step.
  14. The correct answer is D (Certified supplier status, which is earned through sustained, documented, superior performance): Sustained, documented superior performance over time is the basis for certified supplier status, which is the appropriate designation to justify reduced incoming inspection. Option A is wrong because probationary status is applied when performance falls short, which is the opposite of what this supplier has demonstrated. Option B is wrong because disqualified status would remove a high-performing supplier from the approved list, which contradicts their strong record. Option C is wrong because conditional approval with full inspection is typically used for unproven or underperforming suppliers, not a proven strong performer.
  15. The correct answer is C (Ranking risks by combining their likelihood of occurrence with the severity of their impact): Risk prioritization should combine likelihood of occurrence with severity of impact so limited resources are directed at the risks that matter most. Option A is wrong because addressing risks strictly by discovery order ignores their actual likelihood and severity, which may misallocate resources. Option B is wrong because focusing only on risks that already caused disruption ignores high-impact risks that simply have not occurred yet. Option D is wrong because spreading resources equally regardless of likelihood or impact fails to prioritize the risks that matter most.
  16. The correct answer is D (A lack of genuine management commitment beneath the surface level of stated support): Managers who publicly endorse a program while quietly maintaining old practices demonstrate a lack of genuine commitment beneath surface-level support, a classic barrier to sustained quality improvement. Option A is wrong because insufficient statistical training would show up as skill gaps in analysis, not as managers quietly reverting to old practices. Option B is wrong because a shortage of measurement equipment is a resource constraint, not a description of managers privately resisting a new program. Option C is wrong because an aggressive timeline affects the pace of root cause analysis, not whether managers privately continue old practices.
  17. The correct answer is A (Workers may sacrifice quality to hit a volume target, since the quota rewards output over quality): Numerical quotas can push workers to prioritize hitting a volume target over maintaining quality, since the quota structurally rewards output rather than conformance. Option B is wrong because quotas are a management practice choice, not something universally prohibited by labor law in general. Option C is wrong because attributing turnover primarily to quotas overstates their role relative to other well-documented workplace factors, and the effect varies by how quotas are implemented. Option D is wrong because quotas do not fundamentally block the ability to calculate a valid capability index; capability can still be assessed under a quota system, though the underlying data patterns may need closer scrutiny.
  18. The correct answer is B (Regulatory bodies whose requirements the system must demonstrably help the company satisfy): Regulatory bodies are stakeholders because the QMS must help demonstrate compliance with their requirements, even though regulators never log in to or directly use the software. Option A is wrong because limiting stakeholders to IT staff ignores everyone else, including regulators and end users, who have a real interest in the system. Option C is wrong because a vendor's sales representative is not an ongoing stakeholder in the system's operation once the purchase is complete. Option D is wrong because limiting stakeholders only to daily users excludes indirect but important interests like regulatory compliance needs.
  19. The correct answer is D (Holding safety and the public interest paramount even when it conflicts with schedule pressure): The ASQ Code of Ethics requires holding safety and the public interest paramount, which means schedule pressure cannot justify approving a shipment when a required test was skipped. Option A is wrong because deferring entirely to a manager's authority ignores the engineer's independent ethical obligation regarding safety and integrity. Option B is wrong because approving now and documenting concerns only if a problem later occurs fails to prevent a known, avoidable risk upfront. Option C is wrong because splitting the shipment does not resolve the underlying fact that a required test was never actually performed.
  20. The correct answer is A (Ensuring historical data is retained and structured for consistent longitudinal comparison over time): Retaining and structuring historical data for consistent longitudinal comparison is what allows a quality information system to support genuine long-term trend analysis for management review. Option B is wrong because an audible alert on every entry addresses transactional notification, not the historical structure needed for trend analysis. Option C is wrong because matching a public website's color scheme has no bearing on the system's ability to support trend analysis over time. Option D is wrong because retaining only the most recent week of data would eliminate the historical basis needed for any trend analysis at all.
  21. The correct answer is C (Multivoting, where members rank or vote on the generated list to narrow it to top candidates): Multivoting lets the group rank or vote on an already-generated list, which is an effective way to converge on priorities after brainstorming has produced many ideas. Option A is wrong because more brainstorming would generate even more ideas without helping the group agree on which one to pursue, worsening the problem. Option B is wrong because a fishbone diagram is a cause-generation tool, not a technique designed to help a group converge on priorities. Option D is wrong because a checklist audit verifies compliance against known criteria and is not built to help a group reach consensus on priorities.
  22. The correct answer is A (Reduced buy-in and slower adoption since the people affected were not part of the decision): Excluding the team from decisions that affect their daily work typically reduces buy-in and slows adoption, since people are less committed to changes they had no voice in shaping. Option B is wrong because bypassing broader input may speed initial approval but often creates downstream resistance and rework that erodes any early time savings. Option C is wrong because fewer people involved in interpreting data does not by itself improve statistical accuracy of the underlying analysis. Option D is wrong because ISO 9001 does not specifically require or favor unilateral decision-making by a single department head, and documented authority does not depend on excluding team input.
  23. The correct answer is D (Actively listening to the customer's concerns and presenting a specific, timebound corrective plan): Actively listening to the customer and presenting a specific, timebound corrective plan directly addresses the concern while showing the customer the issue is being taken seriously. Option A is wrong because dismissing cosmetic defects as outside scope ignores that they clearly affected the customer's perception of quality. Option B is wrong because an apology alone with no explanation of cause or prevention does nothing to reassure the customer about future shipments. Option C is wrong because redirecting the customer to sales when the concern is a product defect fails to address the underlying quality issue directly.
  24. The correct answer is B (Qualifying at least one alternate supplier who could provide the material if the primary fails): Qualifying at least one alternate supplier directly reduces the plant's exposure to a single point of failure in its raw material supply chain. Option A is wrong because increasing finished goods stock buys time but does not fix the underlying single-source sourcing risk the plan should address. Option C is wrong because renegotiating payment terms addresses cash flow, not the risk that the sole supplier could fail to deliver material at all. Option D is wrong because adding inspection on material from the same sole source does nothing to reduce the risk of that source failing to supply at all.
  25. The correct answer is C (Competitive or industry benchmarking, which compares performance to others in the same sector): Comparing a company's warranty claim rate to a published industry association average for the same product category is a form of competitive or industry benchmarking. Option A is wrong because internal benchmarking compares performance within the same company, not against an external industry-wide average. Option B is wrong because generic benchmarking compares processes across dissimilar industries, which differs from comparing within the same product category. Option D is wrong because strategic benchmarking, as commonly defined, focuses on mission and long-term direction, not a specific operational metric like warranty rate.
  26. The correct answer is B (The plant manager ultimately owns the outcome even though others perform the actual work): Being 'Accountable' in a RACI chart means ultimate ownership of the outcome rests with that person, even though the 'Responsible' engineers actually perform the work. Option A is wrong because accountable does not require personally executing every task; that is the role of those marked Responsible. Option C is wrong because an accountable owner is engaged throughout the activity, not disengaged until it is closed. Option D is wrong because accountable status implies active ownership and oversight, not passive notification only after closure.
  27. The correct answer is A (Facilitating open discussion to clarify roles and resolve conflict constructively): Facilitating open discussion to clarify roles and work through conflict constructively is the appropriate leadership response during the storming stage of team development. Option B is wrong because disbanding a team simply because conflict has surfaced ignores that storming is a normal, expected stage of team development. Option C is wrong because ignoring visible role conflict does not guarantee resolution and often allows the underlying issue to fester. Option D is wrong because removing the most vocal member does not resolve the underlying role conflict and can damage trust in leadership.
  28. The correct answer is B (The targets may not reflect real customer priorities, weakening cross-functional commitment to them): Setting customer satisfaction targets without input from sales or customer service risks targets that do not reflect real customer priorities, weakening cross-functional commitment to achieving them. Option A is wrong because publication in the quality manual does not on its own confer meaningful legitimacy when the targets were developed without cross-functional input. Option C is wrong because the lack of cross-functional input does not reduce the need for a measurement system to track progress against the targets. Option D is wrong because engineering setting targets in isolation does not improve accuracy about real customer priorities; it increases the risk of missing them.
  29. The correct answer is D (The new supplier's demonstrated quality system and process capability for the part in question): A lower price should not override a careful evaluation of the new supplier's demonstrated quality system and process capability for the specific part, since price alone does not predict conformance risk. Option A is wrong because similarity in marketing language reveals nothing meaningful about the new supplier's actual quality capability. Option B is wrong because parking lot size has no bearing on a supplier's process capability or ability to consistently meet specification. Option C is wrong because proximity of headquarters does not indicate whether the new supplier can reliably produce a conforming part.
  30. The correct answer is B (To generate independent ideas before group dynamics or dominant voices can influence thinking): The silent writing step in nominal group technique lets each member generate independent ideas before group dynamics or a dominant voice can shape their thinking. Option A is wrong because the step is not intended to reduce the total number of ideas; it is meant to protect independent idea generation. Option C is wrong because silent writing precedes, rather than replaces, the round-robin sharing step that follows it in the method. Option D is wrong because the method is designed to capture every member's ideas, not to record only the facilitator's own thinking.
  31. The correct answer is C (In-process first-pass yield trends, which can signal emerging issues before products ship): In-process first-pass yield is a leading indicator because shifts in it can signal emerging quality problems before those problems ever reach the customer as a field failure. Option A is wrong because total warranty claims already reflect past field performance, making it a lagging indicator rather than a leading one. Option B is wrong because a satisfaction survey collected months after delivery reports on outcomes that have already occurred, making it lagging. Option D is wrong because recall counts from a prior year describe events that already happened, making this a lagging indicator, not a leading one.
  32. The correct answer is C (A failure to communicate strategy in a way that translates into meaningful daily actions): Employees not understanding how their daily work connects to quality objectives represents a failure to communicate strategy in a way that translates into meaningful daily action. Option A is wrong because the gap described is about understanding and connection, not about whether the objectives were documented in the manual. Option B is wrong because missing a numeric target on each objective is a different issue than employees not understanding the strategic connection. Option D is wrong because failing to benchmark against a competitor is unrelated to employees not understanding their own objectives' purpose.
  33. The correct answer is B (Whether the goal is to map opposing behavioral forces rather than a numeric correlation): The correct tool choice depends on whether the goal is mapping opposing behavioral or organizational forces, which is what force-field analysis is built for, versus a numeric correlation, which a scatter diagram addresses. Option A is wrong because marker color preference has no bearing on which analytical tool correctly fits the team's actual objective. Option C is wrong because whiteboard size is a logistical consideration, not the basis for choosing between two fundamentally different analysis tools. Option D is wrong because a required font for the final report does not determine which analytical tool is appropriate for the underlying question.
  34. The correct answer is A (Each audience needs information framed at the level of detail and relevance to their role): Each audience needs the message framed at the level of detail and relevance appropriate to their role, so shop floor staff and executives typically receive different framing of the same underlying issue. Option B is wrong because there is no general legal prohibition on executives receiving the same operational data as shop floor staff. Option C is wrong because shop floor staff are not broadly barred from ever seeing data that includes a cost figure. Option D is wrong because most organizations do not require a single identical message format for every audience regardless of role or need.
  35. The correct answer is C (Past performance does not guarantee future performance, so status must be periodically reverified): Periodic requalification exists because past performance does not guarantee future performance, so certified status must be periodically reverified to remain meaningful. Option A is wrong because framing requalification as purely administrative ignores its real purpose of confirming ongoing supplier performance. Option B is wrong because even suppliers with a strong historical defect rate can experience process drift, equipment wear, or personnel changes that periodic requalification is designed to catch. Option D is wrong because requalification exists to verify ongoing quality performance, not primarily as a lever for renegotiating pricing.
  36. The correct answer is C (Falsified records can compromise product safety and undermine trust in the entire quality system): Falsified inspection records can compromise product safety and undermine trust in the entire quality system, which is why such issues warrant formal escalation rather than an informal conversation. Option A is wrong because escalation does not by itself determine the outcome; disciplinary decisions depend on the investigation and company policy, not the act of reporting itself. Option B is wrong because handling a safety-relevant integrity issue informally to save perceived time ignores the seriousness of what falsified data represents. Option D is wrong because escalating a concern does not remove the reporter's own ethical obligation to ensure the issue is genuinely addressed; it fulfills that obligation rather than ending it.
  37. The correct answer is D (Untested plans often reveal gaps only when an actual disruption exposes untested assumptions): Regular testing of a business continuity plan is necessary because untested plans often reveal gaps only when an actual disruption exposes assumptions that were never verified in practice. Option A is wrong because testing is valuable regardless of disruption type, not limited only to natural disaster scenarios. Option B is wrong because formal approval of a document does not verify that its procedures will actually work when needed. Option C is wrong because testing exists to validate readiness for a real disruption, not as a way to generate additional billable work.
  38. The correct answer is A (A modest delay in packaging design likely would not delay the overall project completion date): Because the critical path runs through validation testing, a task not on the critical path, like packaging design, typically has schedule float, so a modest delay there would not by itself delay overall completion. Option B is wrong because this overstates the effect of a non-critical-path delay; the critical path concept specifically identifies which delays actually drive the finish date, and packaging design here is not one of them. Option C is wrong because a dependency between packaging design and validation testing is not established simply by which task sits on the critical path in this schedule. Option D is wrong because being off the critical path does not mean a task has been removed from the project scope, only that it currently has schedule slack.
  39. The correct answer is D (Anonymous written input methods that let participants share concerns without attribution): Anonymous written input methods allow participants who fear management reprisal to share honest concerns without attribution, which typically surfaces more truthful root cause information. Option A is wrong because a public roundtable requiring department heads to respond in real time is likely to suppress honest input from fearful participants. Option B is wrong because requiring names and titles on every response is likely to discourage the very honesty the engineer is trying to surface. Option C is wrong because limiting input to already-trusted employees excludes a broader and more representative view of the actual engagement problem.
  40. The correct answer is D (Document the nonconformance and work with the supplier on corrective action while retaining certified status): Documenting the nonconformance and working with the supplier on corrective action while retaining certified status is proportionate to a minor labeling issue that does not affect fit, form, or function. Option A is wrong because revoking certification and requiring full inspection over a minor labeling defect is a disproportionate response given the supplier's status. Option B is wrong because ignoring the nonconformance and skipping documentation fails to track an issue that should still be recorded and addressed even though it is minor. Option C is wrong because terminating a supplier relationship over a single minor labeling issue is a disproportionate response given an otherwise strong record.
  41. The correct answer is A (They help ensure the team considers a broad range of potential cause areas systematically): Predefined categories like Methods, Machines, Materials, and Manpower help ensure the team systematically considers a broad range of potential cause areas rather than fixating on one. Option B is wrong because using categories organizes brainstorming but does not guarantee the true root cause will be identified without further verification. Option C is wrong because categorizing potential causes does not eliminate the need to collect data to confirm which cause is actually responsible. Option D is wrong because the categories organize ideas for discussion; they do not automatically rank causes by statistical significance.
  42. The correct answer is B (Explain the ethical and business risk of closing an unverified complaint and request time to investigate): Explaining the ethical and business risk of closing an unverified complaint, then requesting time to investigate, upholds the obligation to protect customers and the organization rather than dismissing a real concern. Option A is wrong because closing the complaint exactly as instructed despite ethical concerns abandons the engineer's professional obligation to raise the issue. Option C is wrong because investigating in secret without informing the plant manager avoids addressing the disagreement directly and transparently. Option D is wrong because forwarding a customer complaint to a competitor is an inappropriate and unprofessional way to handle the situation.
  43. The correct answer is C (The ability to aggregate quality performance data across processes for periodic executive review): The ability to aggregate quality performance data across processes for periodic executive review directly supports the input needed for an ISO 9001 management review. Option A is wrong because automated birthday reminders serve an unrelated HR function and do not support management review requirements. Option B is wrong because displaying stock price has no relevance to reviewing quality performance data during a management review. Option D is wrong because scheduling a holiday party is an unrelated administrative function with no bearing on management review needs.
  44. The correct answer is A (A mature corrective action process indicates the supplier can identify and prevent recurring problems): A mature, documented corrective action process indicates the supplier can identify and prevent recurring problems, which is a strong predictor of long-term quality reliability. Option B is wrong because a mature, well-used corrective action process is a meaningful indicator of a supplier's ability to prevent recurring problems, not merely paperwork. Option C is wrong because price and delivery alone do not capture whether a supplier can manage and prevent quality problems over time, which is exactly what corrective action maturity reveals. Option D is wrong because corrective action process maturity is relevant to any supplier regardless of the country in which they are located.
  45. The correct answer is C (A prioritization matrix that plots options against impact and effort to guide selection): A prioritization matrix that plots options against impact and effort is specifically designed to help a team decide which competing priorities to tackle first. Option A is wrong because a checksheet tallies defect occurrences and is not designed to compare competing priorities by impact and effort. Option B is wrong because a run chart shows a single variable over time and does not compare multiple competing priorities against each other. Option D is wrong because a histogram shows the distribution of one measurement and is not built to prioritize among several competing options.
  46. The correct answer is D (Sustainable improvement requires resources, culture change, and priority setting that only leadership can provide): Sustainable improvement requires resources, culture change, and priority setting that only leadership has the authority to provide, which is why leadership commitment is central to Deming's philosophy. Option A is wrong because leadership commitment matters across all industries pursuing genuine continuous improvement, not only regulated ones. Option B is wrong because Deming's philosophy places significant responsibility on management and the system, not primarily on frontline operators. Option C is wrong because correct statistical tool use alone cannot substitute for the leadership commitment needed to sustain real improvement.
  47. The correct answer is A (The goals are unlikely to be achieved because accountability and execution mechanisms are missing): Ambitious goals with no assigned owner, timeline, or resources are unlikely to be achieved, since execution requires clear accountability and a mechanism for follow-through. Option B is wrong because strategic plans generally do require meaningful operational detail like ownership and timelines to actually be executed successfully. Option C is wrong because the absence of ownership meaningfully affects whether goals get resourced and pursued, more than this option suggests. Option D is wrong because approval by top management alone does not fully satisfy requirements for effective goal deployment and accountability.
  48. The correct answer is A (It enables the supplier to address root cause, which reduces future complaints from the same source): Sharing complaint trend data with the responsible supplier enables them to address the root cause, which reduces future complaints traceable to that component and benefits the customer relationship. Option B is wrong because sharing data with a supplier does not by itself shift meaningful legal liability for a defect away from the company. Option C is wrong because sharing information does not ensure contract renewal, which depends on many broader relationship factors. Option D is wrong because sharing data with a supplier does not remove the ongoing need to track and trend future customer complaints.
  49. The correct answer is B (Develop actions that strengthen driving forces and reduce or remove restraining forces): After identifying driving and restraining forces, the team should develop actions that strengthen the driving forces and reduce or remove the restraining ones to enable the change. Option A is wrong because restraining forces like lack of training are critical barriers that must still be addressed, not set aside from consideration. Option C is wrong because ranking only driving forces by cost ignores the restraining forces that could otherwise block successful adoption. Option D is wrong because ending the session after identification alone skips the action-planning step that gives the analysis practical value.
  50. The correct answer is B (Investigate the tip thoroughly through proper channels regardless of its anonymous source): Investigating the tip thoroughly through proper channels, regardless of its anonymous origin, reflects the ethical obligation to protect the public and the integrity of the profession. Option A is wrong because automatically dismissing anonymous tips risks ignoring a legitimate integrity concern that could affect product safety. Option C is wrong because publicly announcing an unverified accusation before investigation is unfair and inconsistent with due process. Option D is wrong because asking the accused individual to investigate themselves creates an obvious conflict of interest in the process.
  51. The correct answer is B (The plan lacked an alternate site or capacity strategy for a single-facility disruption scenario): The flood exposes that the plan lacked an alternate site or capacity strategy for the scenario where the single assumed facility becomes unavailable. Option A is wrong because the plan's failure here is a lack of contingency planning, not an issue of having too much redundancy built in. Option C is wrong because the flood demonstrates a specific planning gap; it does not show that business continuity planning cannot meaningfully address disaster scenarios in general. Option D is wrong because the core weakness is the missing alternate-site strategy, not the inability to anticipate the exact timing of the event.
  52. The correct answer is D (Involving team members with direct process knowledge in decisions that affect their own work): Involving team members with direct process knowledge in decisions affecting their own work typically improves both decision quality and team performance compared with unilateral decision-making. Option A is wrong because continuing unilateral decisions while explaining afterward does not give the team a genuine voice in the decision itself. Option B is wrong because delegating only minor decisions while retaining all major ones still excludes the team from the decisions that matter most. Option C is wrong because requiring written requests before discussion adds friction and does not meaningfully involve the team in the decision process.
  53. The correct answer is A (A gap between documented practice and actual daily practice, undermining the system's intent): A documented procedure that is rarely followed on the floor illustrates a gap between documented practice and actual daily practice, which undermines the entire system's intent even when documentation exists. Option B is wrong because this scenario explicitly involves an existing, documented procedure, not the absence of a written one. Option C is wrong because the scenario as described involves human practice deviating from documentation, not a software or printing failure. Option D is wrong because the scenario is about the procedure not being followed, not about the procedure being made obsolete by regulation.
  54. The correct answer is A (Assessing the supplier's process capability and quality system maturity before any order is placed): Assessing a supplier's process capability and quality system maturity before placing any order is a proactive, risk-based approach that reduces the chance of quality problems later. Option B is wrong because waiting for the first shipment to judge quality is a purely reactive approach that risks receiving nonconforming parts. Option C is wrong because choosing based only on response speed to a bid request ignores the supplier's actual capability to deliver quality. Option D is wrong because selecting based only on payment terms ignores whether the supplier can actually meet the required quality standards.
  55. The correct answer is C (Presenting the data along with root cause context and a proposed corrective action plan): Presenting the data along with root cause context and a proposed corrective action plan gives leadership the full picture needed to make an informed decision at management review. Option A is wrong because presenting only positive metrics hides a known issue from leadership, undermining the purpose of an honest management review. Option B is wrong because presenting a decline with no supporting data leaves leadership unable to judge its true significance or cause. Option D is wrong because delaying the presentation until after the review has concluded defeats the purpose of informing leadership in time to act.
  56. The correct answer is C (Drive out fear so that employees can report problems without risk of blame): Deming's point on driving out fear addresses exactly this situation: punishing workers for a system-caused defect, worn tooling maintenance had not replaced, teaches people to hide problems rather than report them, which blinds management to the real cause.
  57. The correct answer is B (Identify the critical few objectives that link directly to the broader strategic goals): The correct answer identifies critical-few objectives first because a strategic roadmap must be built on measurable links between the QMS and the organization's actual strategic goals before any resources, tools, or reassignments are committed.
  58. The correct answer is C (It describes the system's scope and how the organization's policies and processes interact to meet requirements.): The quality manual sits at the top of the documentation hierarchy and provides an overview of the quality system's scope, the organization's quality policy, and how the major processes interact to satisfy applicable requirements. Procedures beneath it describe how specific processes are carried out, work instructions provide step-by-step detail for individual tasks, and records provide objective evidence that the system was followed.   121. The correct answer is C (Document control, since it should have ensured only the current work instruction revision was in use.): Document control is the component of the quality system responsible for ensuring that only the current, approved version of a document is available at the point of use, and that obsolete versions are removed or clearly marked. Finding an outdated work instruction still in active use six months after supersession indicates that the document control process, not the work instruction itself, failed to function as intended, since the revision may have been correct when issued.   122. The correct answer is D (A record provides evidence an activity actually occurred, while a procedure describes how to perform it.): A procedure is a forward-looking document that describes the intended method for carrying out a process, such as how incoming material is to be inspected. A record is a backward-looking document that provides objective evidence of what actually happened, such as a completed inspection report showing the specific results obtained. This distinction matters because a procedure can be well written and approved, yet records may later reveal it was not followed in practice. Option A is wrong because a procedure and a record serve fundamentally different purposes, one prescriptive and one evidentiary.   123. The correct answer is D (Personnel have no reliable way to confirm which document revision is currently authorized for use.): The master list, or an equivalent document register, is the mechanism that allows anyone in the organization to verify which revision of a controlled document is currently valid without having to track down the document owner individually. Without it, different departments or shifts could reasonably be using different revisions of the same document with no independent way to detect the discrepancy, undermining the consistency the document control system is meant to guarantee.   124. The correct answer is C (The approved engineering drawing or specification, since production must not deviate from an unapproved baseline.): Configuration control exists to ensure that a product is built and maintained to a single, currently authorized baseline of drawings and specifications. Before a tolerance change can be implemented on the production floor, the engineering drawing or specification itself must be formally revised and approved through the change control process, since allowing production to deviate from an unapproved baseline breaks traceability between what was built and what was authorized. Downstream documents are then updated to reflect the new baseline. Option A is wrong because the purchase order defines commercial terms, not the engineering baseline governing tolerances. Option B is wrong because training records are updated later but do not authorize the engineering change itself.   125. The correct answer is A (Tracking every approved change against a baseline so the built product can be traced to it.): Configuration control's core purpose is to prevent drift between what a product actually is and what its governing documentation says it should be. This is achieved by establishing a baseline of approved drawings, specifications, and revisions, and then requiring every subsequent change to be formally evaluated, approved, and recorded against that baseline before it is implemented. Without this traceable link, an organization loses the ability to know with confidence which configuration is actually in the field. Option B is wrong because unreviewed line changes break the traceability configuration control is meant to preserve. Option C is wrong because unlabeled versions provide no way to distinguish the current authorized revision. Option D is wrong because configuration control applies to hardware and software together, not to software alone.
  126. The correct answer is D (The lack of a single controlled point that guarantees everyone accesses the current document revision.): Distributing a document update through informal email rather than a controlled system means there is no guarantee that every recipient actually replaced their old copy, and no mechanism to prevent someone outside the original distribution list from continuing to use an obsolete version they already had. A properly controlled document system uses a single authoritative location from which the current revision is always retrieved, rather than relying on push distribution that cannot be verified as complete. Option A is wrong because an audit might catch the problem, but the root weakness is the distribution method. Option B is wrong because the scenario describes a distribution failure, not necessarily an error in technical content. Option C is wrong because the failure is in document distribution and control, not in reading ability.
  127. The correct answer is B (Requiring a change request to be evaluated for impact on interfacing components before approval.): Effective configuration control treats a proposed change as something that can ripple beyond the single part being modified, so an impact assessment on interfacing components, tooling, and documentation is performed before approval, not after implementation. This prevents a change intended to solve one problem from unintentionally creating a fit, form, or function issue elsewhere in the assembly. Approving changes without this cross-impact review is a common source of downstream nonconformances. Option A is wrong because implementing before evaluation removes the preventive control configuration management provides. Option C is wrong because ignoring interfacing components risks introducing new fit, form, or function problems. Option D is wrong because a documented, traceable approval record is required to maintain an auditable link.
  128. The correct answer is B (Evaluate the change against the approved specification and baseline before accepting any parts made under it.): Because configuration control depends on the product matching its authorized baseline, an unapproved change by the supplier, even if well-intentioned, breaks that link until it has been formally evaluated. The customer's quality function must assess whether the changed dimensions still meet the approved specification and whether the change affects form, fit, or function, and formally approve or reject it, before any parts made under the new process are accepted into inventory or used in production. Option A is wrong because voluntary disclosure does not substitute for formal evaluation against the baseline.   129. Recording this information at the time of manufacture, rather than relying on the current revision level, is essential because documents may have changed multiple times since that unit was built, and a field investigation needs the historical configuration, not the present one.   131. The correct answer is A (ISO 9001 specifies certifiable requirements, while ISO 9000 gives vocabulary and ISO 9004 gives sustained-success guidance.): Within the ISO 9000 family, ISO 9001 is the only document that contains auditable requirements against which an organization's quality management system can be certified by a third party. ISO 9000 itself is not a requirements document; it establishes fundamental concepts and defines the vocabulary used consistently across the family. ISO 9004 goes beyond the minimum requirements of ISO 9001 to provide guidance for organizations pursuing sustained, long-term success, but it is not used for certification. Option B is wrong because this reverses the roles; ISO 9001 holds the requirements and ISO 9000 gives vocabulary.   132. The correct answer is A (It requires determining risks and opportunities affecting conformity and planning proportionate actions to address them.): ISO 9001 embeds risk-based thinking throughout the standard rather than treating it as a separate, standalone clause, requiring organizations to identify risks and opportunities relevant to the quality management system's ability to achieve its intended results and to plan proportionate actions to address them. This replaces the older approach of relying solely on a stand-alone preventive action clause, integrating risk consideration into planning, operations, and performance evaluation instead. Option B is wrong because ISO 9001 requires risk-based thinking but no specific quantified scoring methodology. Option C is wrong because risk-based thinking is an ongoing requirement, not satisfied once at initial certification.   133. The correct answer is B (No, because ISO 9001 certifies a documented management system exists, not a specific defect rate.): ISO 9001 certification confirms that an organization has established and is operating a quality management system that meets the standard's requirements, including elements such as process control, documentation, and continual improvement. It does not certify or guarantee a specific product defect rate, since actual product outcomes depend on many factors beyond the presence of a management system, including process capability and material variation. A quality engineer should communicate this distinction clearly to avoid overstating certification. Option A is wrong because certification addresses the management system, not a legal guarantee of zero defects. Option C is wrong because certification carries no calendar-year defect guarantee tied to when products ship. Option D is wrong because ISO 9001 is applicable to manufacturing as well as service and other sectors.
  134. The correct answer is C (The process approach organizes the system as interrelated processes, managed together via plan-do-check-act.): ISO 9001 structures its requirements around the process approach, which views the quality management system as a network of interrelated processes that transform inputs into outputs to deliver intended results. The plan-do-check-act cycle is the management method applied both to individual processes and to the system overall, ensuring that resources are planned, processes are implemented, performance is monitored, and opportunities for improvement are acted upon in a continuous cycle. Option A is wrong because the two concepts are complementary and used together, not one replacing the other. Option B is wrong because the process approach applies broadly, not solely to management review. Option D is wrong because plan-do-check-act is a management method used in normal operations, not just audits.
  135. The correct answer is C (Establishing a process to monitor customer perceptions and feeding that data into management review.): ISO 9001 requires the organization to monitor customer perceptions of the degree to which their needs and expectations have been fulfilled, and to determine the methods for obtaining and using that information. Feeding this customer perception data into management review, alongside other performance data, allows leadership to evaluate whether the quality management system is actually delivering the intended outcome of customer satisfaction and to direct improvement actions accordingly. Option A is wrong because a displayed certificate does not itself generate or measure customer satisfaction data. Option B is wrong because relying mainly on formal written complaints misses the broader perception data intended between complaints. Option D is wrong because monitoring customer perception is a quality system requirement needing genuine quality department involvement, not just limited coordination from marketing.
  136. The correct answer is B (The quality policy must provide a framework for objectives and align with strategic direction.): ISO 9001 requires top management to establish a quality policy that is appropriate to the purpose and context of the organization and that supports its strategic direction, because the policy provides the framework within which measurable quality objectives are subsequently set. A policy that is disconnected from strategic direction risks producing quality objectives that do not actually support what the organization is trying to achieve, undermining the intended linkage between top-level commitment and operational quality activity. Option A is wrong because the quality policy is a required element under ISO 9001, not optional. Option C is wrong because ISO 9001 assigns responsibility for the quality policy to top management, not customers. Option D is wrong because the policy is expected to address quality outcomes directly, not treat them as secondary to safety content.
  137. The correct answer is D (To provide a comprehensive self-assessment framework for improving overall performance and competitiveness, not just quality management.): Unlike ISO 9001, which specifies certifiable requirements for a quality management system, the Baldrige criteria are designed as a broad, non-prescriptive self-assessment framework that examines leadership, strategy, customers, measurement and analysis, workforce, operations, and results together. Applying the framework helps an organization identify strengths and opportunities for improvement across its entire management system, with the goal of improving overall performance and competitiveness rather than achieving a pass or fail certification outcome. Option A is wrong because Baldrige is a recognition and self-assessment framework, not a legally required certification. Option B is wrong because the framework complements rather than replaces a quality management system. Option C is wrong because the criteria explicitly include leadership and workforce alongside results, not results alone.
  138. The correct answer is B (Results are evaluated as the outcome of performance across the other categories in an integrated way.): The Baldrige framework treats the Results category as the culmination of performance in the other categories, meaning that strong results are expected to reflect effective leadership, sound strategic planning, customer focus, workforce engagement, and well-managed operations working together. Evaluators look for this integration, examining whether an organization's approaches in the other categories are actually deployed and producing the results being reported, rather than evaluating results in isolation from how they were achieved. Option A is wrong because the framework specifically links results to the approaches described in other categories. Option C is wrong because the Results category includes customer, workforce, and operational measures too. Option D is wrong because all Baldrige categories carry meaningful scoring weight; results are significant but the other categories are far more than a minor factor.
  139. The correct answer is D (The soundness and appropriateness of the methods the organization uses to meet that category item's requirements.): In Baldrige scoring, 'Approach' examines the methods an organization uses to address a category item's requirements, including how well those methods are aligned with organizational needs and whether they are systematic rather than reactive or ad hoc. This is distinct from 'Deployment,' which examines how consistently the approach is applied across relevant work units, and from 'Learning' and 'Integration,' which examine refinement over time and alignment with other organizational needs. Option A is wrong because training certificate counts are a workforce metric, not what Approach evaluates. Option B is wrong because revenue is a results measure and unrelated to evaluating an approach's soundness. Option C is wrong because prior application history is not a factor the Approach dimension assesses.
  140. The correct answer is A (Baldrige evaluates the overall management system and performance, while an ISO 9001 audit verifies defined requirements.): An ISO 9001 audit is a conformance-based activity that checks whether specific, defined requirements of the standard have been met and are being followed, resulting in a certification decision. The Baldrige framework instead provides a broader, criteria-based self-assessment covering leadership, strategy, customers, measurement, workforce, operations, and results, intended to drive organizational improvement and role-model performance rather than to determine pass or fail conformance to a fixed requirement set. Option B is wrong because the two frameworks differ in scope and outcome, one conformance-based, one broader. Option C is wrong because Baldrige has criteria for business, healthcare, education, and nonprofit sectors alike. Option D is wrong because Baldrige includes dedicated leadership and workforce categories with more depth than an ISO audit.
  141. The correct answer is C (The organization has sound methods, but they are not consistently applied across all relevant work units.): A gap between strong Approach and weak Deployment scores is a common and telling pattern in Baldrige feedback, indicating that the organization has done well in designing systematic, well-reasoned methods for addressing category requirements, but has not achieved consistent application of those methods throughout the organization, such as across different departments, shifts, or locations. This points to an implementation and consistency problem rather than a design problem, directing improvement effort toward rollout and reinforcement. Option A is wrong because a strong Approach score specifically indicates that documented, sound methods do exist. Option B is wrong because the pattern described concerns Approach versus Deployment, not financial results. Option D is wrong because a consistent gap across multiple categories is a meaningful pattern, not an error.
  142. The correct answer is A (A product audit, since it evaluates the product itself against its specified requirements.): A product audit examines a specific product or a sample of product against its applicable specifications, drawings, or other requirements to determine conformance, typically as a check independent of the process that produced it. This differs from a process audit, which examines whether the manufacturing or service process itself is being carried out according to its documented procedures, regardless of whether any specific unit of output is checked. Option B is wrong because a process audit evaluates manufacturing steps, while this scenario evaluates finished product. Option C is wrong because a management audit evaluates leadership at a higher level, not a batch of product. Option D is wrong because a registration audit is performed by a certification body, not to check one batch.
  143. The correct answer is A (A process audit, because it evaluates conformance of the process activities themselves to the documented procedure.): A process audit is focused on the method by which work is performed, verifying that the actual sequence of steps, equipment parameters, and controls in use match what is documented and approved, independent of whether a specific unit of output happens to conform. This is distinct from a product audit, which evaluates the output itself, since a process could be followed exactly as documented and still occasionally produce a nonconforming unit due to other variation sources. Option B is wrong because a product audit checks the output item, whereas this scenario checks the process steps. Option C is wrong because a compliance audit addresses regulatory requirements, while this checks an internal procedure. Option D is wrong because first-party audit refers to who performs it, not who is permitted to check a process.
  144. The correct answer is B (A registration audit performed by a third party, since the certification body is independent.): A registration audit, sometimes called a certification or surveillance audit, is conducted by an accredited certification body to determine whether an organization's quality management system continues to meet the requirements of a recognized standard, in order to grant or maintain certification. Because the certification body has no ownership stake in the supplier's business and is not the supplier's direct customer, it is classified as a third-party audit, distinct from a first-party or second-party audit. Option A is wrong because a first-party audit is conducted by the organization itself, not an independent body. Option C is wrong because a second-party audit is conducted by a customer on its supplier, not a certifier. Option D is wrong because the scenario describes a management system audit for certification, not one batch.
  145. The correct answer is B (A nonconformance against the calibration procedure, since the required interval was not followed for that gauge.): The exhibit's finding section states that the digital caliper's calibration record shows a due date that passed forty-two days before the audit, with the gauge still in active use on the line, which is a direct nonconformance to the calibration procedure's stated requirement that gauges be recalibrated by their due date or removed from service. The evidence section confirms this with the specific record reviewed and the observed gap, giving the auditee the specific detail needed to investigate why the interval was missed rather than a vague or general statement. Option A is wrong because the exhibit's requirement section states calibration by the due date is a stated requirement. Option C is wrong because the exhibit cites the calibration procedure, not the design control process, as the source. Option D is wrong because the exhibit documents a gauge used past its due date, which is a nonconformance.
  146. The correct answer is B (A second-party audit, because the customer organization is auditing its own supplier.): A second-party audit occurs when an organization audits one of its suppliers, contractors, or other external parties with which it has a direct business relationship, typically to evaluate the supplier's ability to meet the customer's requirements before or during the supply relationship. This differs from a first-party audit, which the manufacturer would conduct on its own internal processes, and from a third-party audit conducted by an independent certification body unrelated to either party. Option A is wrong because a first-party audit is an organization's self-audit, not an audit of an external supplier. Option C is wrong because a third-party audit is performed by a certification body, not the customer itself. Option D is wrong because the scenario describes process controls, not specifically leadership structure.
  147. The correct answer is A (It evaluates whether management practices, such as planning and resource allocation, effectively support objectives.): A management audit examines the effectiveness of management-level activities and decisions, such as how objectives are set, resources are allocated, and performance is reviewed, to determine whether the management system as a whole is functioning in a way that supports organizational goals. This is a broader, higher-level focus than a process audit, which examines the conformance of a specific operational process, or a product audit, which examines a specific product against its requirements. Option B is wrong because dimensional conformance is the focus of a product audit, not a management audit. Option C is wrong because reviewing production step sequence is characteristic of a process audit instead. Option D is wrong because a management audit can be performed internally as a first-party audit as well.
  148. The correct answer is A (A compliance audit, verifying adherence to applicable laws rather than a voluntary standard.): A compliance audit is specifically oriented toward verifying that an organization is meeting applicable legal, regulatory, or contractual requirements, such as government manufacturing regulations, rather than evaluating conformance to a voluntary consensus standard or an internal procedure. While a compliance audit can share techniques with a process or product audit, its defining characteristic is that the criteria being audited against originate from a legal or regulatory source rather than the organization's own system alone. Option B is wrong because the scenario describes a regulatory agency conducting the audit, not self-scheduling. Option C is wrong because the scenario describes broad regulatory verification, not narrowly one finished product. Option D is wrong because the scenario describes regulatory compliance verification, not voluntary certification.
  149. The correct answer is B (A first-party audit, because the organization is auditing its own process using its own personnel.): A first-party audit, often called an internal audit, is conducted by an organization on its own processes and systems, typically using its own trained personnel or personnel it designates, to verify ongoing conformance to its own documented requirements and to identify opportunities for improvement before an external party might find the same gaps. This is distinct from a second-party audit conducted by a customer and a third-party audit conducted by an independent certification body. Option A is wrong because the scenario describes the organization auditing itself, with no customer representative. Option C is wrong because using its own personnel makes this a first-party audit regardless of later use. Option D is wrong because the scenario verifies against the organization's own procedure, not a regulation.
  150. The correct answer is D (Request or assign a technical expert or another auditor with relevant expertise for that portion.): Audit team composition is expected to ensure that the team collectively has the competence needed to evaluate the processes within the audit scope, and a lead auditor who recognizes a gap in technical expertise during the audit should arrange for a subject matter expert or qualified auditor to support that specific area rather than proceeding without adequate competence. Continuing without the needed expertise risks drawing incorrect conclusions about conformance and undermines the credibility of the audit findings. Option A is wrong because silently omitting a process from the report misrepresents the actual scope covered. Option B is wrong because drawing conclusions from a general impression undermines audit objectivity and rigor. Option C is wrong because relying on self-certification removes the independent verification an audit provides.
  151. The correct answer is D (Determining the audit's objectives, scope, and criteria, and initiating the request for the audit itself.): The audit client is the organization or person that requests the audit and is therefore responsible for defining why the audit is being conducted, what its objectives and scope should be, and against which criteria the auditee will be evaluated. The lead auditor is responsible for planning and conducting the audit within that defined scope, while the auditee is responsible for providing access, information, and resources needed to carry out the audit and for addressing any findings afterward. Option A is wrong because escorting the team through the facility is typically an auditee responsibility. Option B is wrong because collecting evidence and conducting interviews is the audit team's responsibility. Option C is wrong because implementing corrective actions in response to findings is the auditee's responsibility.
  152. The correct answer is C (Remain objective, continue presenting factual evidence, and avoid a personal or emotional dispute with the owner.): An auditor's role is to gather objective evidence and compare it against the audit criteria, not to win an argument or force agreement in the moment. Remaining calm, continuing to reference specific factual evidence such as records or observed conditions, and avoiding escalation into a personal dispute preserves the auditor's objectivity and the credibility of the audit, while still allowing the process owner an opportunity to explain or provide additional evidence that might change the auditor's conclusion. Option A is wrong because ending the audit and escalating to human resources is disproportionate to a disagreement. Option B is wrong because removing a valid finding to avoid conflict compromises the audit's objectivity. Option D is wrong because matching a heated tone undermines the auditor's objectivity and professionalism.
  153. The correct answer is D (Providing access to relevant areas, personnel, and records, and cooperating so objective evidence can be gathered.): The auditee's core responsibility is to support the audit process by granting access to the relevant work areas, making appropriate personnel available for interview, and providing the records and documents the audit team needs to gather objective evidence. This cooperative role allows the audit team to reach conclusions based on accurate, complete evidence rather than incomplete information, and it is distinct from the audit team's own responsibility to plan the audit and reach independent conclusions. Option A is wrong because the audit team determines which findings are included based on the evidence, not the auditee. Option B is wrong because scope and objectives are determined by the audit client, not by the auditee alone. Option C is wrong because opening and closing meetings are conducted jointly with the audit team present.
  154. The correct answer is D (Risk-based audit planning, since audit resources are allocated according to the relative risk each supplier poses.): Risk-based audit planning allocates limited audit time and resources toward the areas, processes, or suppliers that pose the greatest risk to the organization, based on factors such as past nonconformance history, the criticality of the parts or processes involved, and performance trends. This ensures that higher-risk suppliers receive more frequent or more thorough scrutiny than lower-risk ones, rather than auditing every supplier on an identical fixed schedule regardless of their actual risk profile. Option A is wrong because the scenario explicitly describes selection based on defined risk criteria, not chance. Option B is wrong because the scenario describes prioritizing suppliers to audit, not verifying closed findings. Option C is wrong because the scenario describes audit prioritization, not a management review meeting.
  155. The correct answer is B (Department B.): Because its assessment score is lowest despite a high completion rate. The table shows Department B with a 98 percent training completion rate but only a 62 percent post-training competency assessment score, the lowest score of the three departments, indicating that most employees attended the course but a much smaller share can actually demonstrate the required skill afterward. Department A and Department C both show assessment scores well above 80 percent alongside comparable completion rates, suggesting the training translated more effectively into demonstrated competency in those two departments. Completion rate alone, as shown for Department B, does not indicate that learning transfer actually occurred.   156. The correct answer is D (The specific processes, areas, and criteria to be audited, along with the schedule and team assignments.): An effective audit plan defines the scope of what will actually be examined, including the specific processes and areas covered and the criteria, such as applicable procedures or standards, against which conformance will be judged, together with a schedule and clear assignment of responsibilities among the audit team. Providing this information in advance allows the auditee to make appropriate personnel and records available and allows the audit team to conduct a focused, efficient audit rather than an unstructured review. Option A is wrong because predetermining findings before gathering evidence would bias the audit outcome. Option B is wrong because personal performance reviews are unrelated to and do not belong in an audit plan.
