The correct answer is B (Traceability may be lost or confused, since the card no longer matches the actual contents): moving parts between totes without updating the identification card breaks the link between the card and the actual contents, creating confusion or lost traceability. Claiming the card will still be accurate contradicts the very scenario described, where contents were moved without updating it. The assumption that transferring parts improves traceability accuracy is incorrect, since moving contents without updating records only increases confusion. Dismissing the practice as having little effect ignores that totes used for staging still require accurate identification while material is in them.
  42. The correct answer is C (The ability to link the raw material lot forward to every finished good it was used in): forward traceability from a raw material lot to every finished good produced with it is exactly what is needed to execute a targeted recall if contamination is reported. A general description of packaging methods does nothing to identify which specific finished goods are affected. Total headcount for the quarter is unrelated to tracing material through the process. Listing marketing materials distributed with the product has no bearing on identifying which lots need to be recalled.
  43. The correct answer is A (Color-blind personnel or faded tags may cause the status to be misread or misapplied): relying on color alone risks misinterpretation by color-blind personnel or when a tag fades, so a written or coded designation should accompany the color to ensure the status is read correctly. Cost of producing colored tags is not the primary risk being described. Color coding is not universally standardized across every facility, so misinterpretation remains a real possibility. Color tags alone do not remove the need for a supporting written record of material disposition.
  44. The correct answer is D (Ensuring older conforming lots are consumed before newer lots while preserving lot traceability): combining first-in-first-out flow with lot-based labeling supports using older conforming material first while still preserving the ability to trace each lot through the process. Prioritizing the newest material first contradicts the entire purpose of a first-in-first-out system. Label font requirements are a cosmetic detail unrelated to the material control objective being described. Requiring every lot to sit in the exact same physical location is impractical and not the goal of this combination of controls.
  45. The correct answer is C (Physically separate the nonconforming casting into a clearly marked hold area away from conforming stock): the immediate action required is physical segregation of the nonconforming casting into a marked hold area, which prevents accidental use regardless of when disposition is finalized. Leaving the casting in the bin until disposition is decided creates ongoing risk of accidental use in the meantime. Relying on the next operator to visually catch the nonconforming casting is not a reliable substitute for physical segregation. Mixing the casting further into the bin makes accidental use more, not less, likely.
  46. The correct answer is B (A tag alone can be removed or overlooked, allowing accidental use of suspect material): a locked cage adds a physical barrier that a tag alone cannot provide, since tags can be removed, fall off, or simply be overlooked by a busy operator. A locked cage does not reduce the need for further quality inspection of the material inside it. Building code compliance is not the stated purpose of using a locked cage for nonconforming material control. Cost comparison between a tag and a cage is irrelevant to the actual risk being addressed.
  47. The correct answer is D (Suspect and released material sharing a single pallet without segregation creates a risk of comingling): shipping suspect and released material together on one pallet with no separation creates a genuine risk that the two lots become comingled and indistinguishable. Mixed pallets of this kind are not a preferred or acceptable shipping practice when one lot is under investigation. Segregation remains necessary specifically because one of the two lots has not cleared the investigation, regardless of the other lot's release status. Sharing a raw material heat does not eliminate the need to segregate a lot that is currently suspect.
  48. The correct answer is A (Physical barriers, distinct signage, and restricted access documented in a controlled procedure): an effective hold area combines physical barriers, clear signage, and restricted access under a controlled procedure to keep nonconforming material isolated until disposition. An unmarked shelf near the shipping dock provides no real barrier against accidental shipment. A verbal reminder at shift start is not a durable or auditable control method. Sharing floor space with finished goods awaiting shipment increases, rather than reduces, the risk of accidental release.
  49. The correct answer is B (Distinct tagging and separate physical locations should be established for rework versus scrap): rework and scrap require distinct tagging and separate physical locations so that material intended for repair is never mistakenly destroyed and scrap is never mistakenly reworked. Leaving identical red tags in place does nothing to distinguish the two very different dispositions. Combining rework and scrap into one category erases a meaningful distinction the plant needs for correct handling. Removing the tags entirely would make the two categories even harder to tell apart.
  50. The correct answer is C (Relocate nonconforming stock to a distinctly separated, clearly marked area away from line-side bins): moving nonconforming stock to a clearly separated and marked area away from the conforming supply bin directly reduces the chance of accidental installation, addressing the root cause of the near-miss. Adding a general sign about the assembly line's purpose does nothing to prevent parts from being confused at the point of use. Increasing production rate would reduce, not increase, the time operators have to notice a problem. Combining conforming and nonconforming stock into one bin would make the exact near-miss more likely to recur.
  51. The correct answer is D (The classification is based on the potential severity of harm or failure the defect could cause): defect classification is driven by the severity of consequence a defect could produce, which is why a structural crack is treated as far more serious than a cosmetic scratch. Which department discovers a defect has no bearing on its actual severity classification. The order in which defects appear during production does not determine their classification level. Physical size alone does not capture consequence, since a small crack in a load-bearing part can be far more serious than a larger cosmetic mark.
  52. The correct answer is A (A tighter AQL for critical defects reflects the greater consequence of allowing them to pass): a tighter acceptance quality limit for critical defects reflects the much greater consequence of letting a critical defect escape compared with a minor cosmetic issue. Administrative cost of the sampling plan is not the reasoning behind setting different AQL values by defect class. AQL values by class are set deliberately based on consequence, not arbitrarily. Supplier requests for faster turnaround are not a valid basis for setting a critical defect AQL.
  53. The correct answer is C (The font size issue should be classified as a lower-severity defect than the missing safety warning): a minor cosmetic labeling issue where all required information remains legible carries far less consequence than a missing safety warning, so it should be classified at a lower severity level. Treating both nonconformances as receiving comparable disposition ignores the real difference in consequence between the two situations. Claiming the font issue is more severe because it affects every unit misapplies frequency as if it were the same thing as severity. Labeling nonconformances still require classification since labeling can carry real safety and regulatory significance.
  54. The correct answer is B (Specific, illustrated examples and clear criteria defining each defect classification level): adding illustrated examples and clearly written criteria for each classification level gives inspectors a consistent, objective basis for borderline decisions. Allowing each inspector to define levels by personal preference is precisely the inconsistency the guide needs to eliminate. Collapsing every nonconformance into a single classification level removes the distinction needed to prioritize response appropriately. Removing written criteria entirely would make consistent classification even less achievable than before.
  55. The correct answer is D (As a minor defect, since it does not impair the intended use of the housing): a visible cosmetic defect that does not affect fit, form, or function is appropriately classified as minor, since it has no impact on the part's intended use. Treating a visible mark as generally threatening product safety overstates the consequence of a defect with no safety implication. Requiring a prompt line shutdown despite the defect's limited actual functional impact is a disproportionate response. Claiming short shots are rarely documented in any standard ignores that classification guides commonly cover cosmetic molding defects like this one.
  56. The correct answer is A (Scrapping the part immediately without any evaluation of possible rework or use-as-is options): jumping straight to scrap without evaluating rework or use-as-is options is inconsistent with a major, non-safety classification, which typically warrants a fuller disposition review first. Routing the part through the standard hold and evaluation process is exactly what the major classification calls for. Reviewing the part against engineering drawings before recommending disposition supports, rather than contradicts, a proper major-defect review. Documenting the nonconformance and its classification is a necessary and consistent step regardless of the final disposition chosen.
  57. The correct answer is C (Apply the customer's specified classification criteria for parts shipped under that contract): when a customer specification defines its own classification criteria, the supplier must apply those criteria to parts produced under that contract, since the customer's requirements govern. Applying the internal system even when it conflicts with customer requirements would violate the contractual obligation to the customer's specification. Alternating between two systems based on shift produces inconsistent and unreliable classification decisions. Ignoring both systems in favor of subjective visual judgment abandons the documented criteria both parties rely on.
  58. The correct answer is D (Determining whether the nonconforming condition affects fit, form, function, or safety): the core responsibility of the MRB is to evaluate a nonconforming condition against fit, form, function, and safety to determine an appropriate disposition. Approving an invoice for the machining operation is unrelated to the technical disposition decision at hand. Reassigning the operator is a personnel matter separate from the MRB's evaluation of the nonconforming lot itself. Scheduling preventive maintenance is a separate maintenance activity, not part of the disposition decision the MRB must make.
  59. The correct answer is B (Repair restores the part to a usable condition without fully meeting original specification): a repair disposition restores a part to a usable condition even though it does not fully conform to the original specification, whereas rework brings the part into full conformance with the print. Claiming the two terms are identical ignores this meaningful distinction used in MRB decision-making. Repair is not reliably cheaper than scrap in most situations, since cost depends on the specific defect and process involved. Repair is used based on technical feasibility, not on whether the customer happens to be unavailable.
  60. The correct answer is A (Documented engineering justification and, where required, customer or regulatory concurrence): a use-as-is disposition needs documented engineering justification and, when applicable, formal customer or regulatory concurrence before it can be finalized and implemented. A verbal agreement with no written record fails to provide the documented justification a use-as-is disposition requires. The assumption that functional test results alone eliminate the need for further review overlooks the engineering justification and any required approvals still needed. Deciding to scrap the entire lot despite a valid use-as-is recommendation contradicts the disposition the engineer already determined was appropriate.
  61. The correct answer is C (The scrapped material could be mistakenly shipped or reused without a documented trail): failing to log an MRB scrap decision against the affected lot number creates a real risk that the material could later be mistakenly released, shipped, or reused since no documented trail exists to prevent it. Claiming an MRB decision is final the moment it is spoken ignores the need for a durable, auditable record. Verbal decisions are not inherently more reliable than documented ones and are actually more prone to being lost or forgotten. Reducing paperwork by skipping the log entry undermines traceability rather than improving it.
  62. The correct answer is D (The board must obtain documented customer approval for the deviation before implementation): when a contract requires customer approval for any print deviation, the MRB must obtain documented customer approval before the use-as-is disposition can be implemented. Claiming MRB authority overrides contract terms disregards the customer's contractual approval rights. Treating scrap as the default step because approval is assumed unavailable ignores that customer approval can and should be requested through the proper channel. Shipping the lot and only notifying the customer if a complaint arises violates the contractual requirement for prior approval.
  63. The correct answer is B (A requirement that corrective action be initiated when a nonconformance recurs at the same location): a recurring hole-position nonconformance dispositioned repeatedly as rework without investigation signals a need to require corrective action whenever the same nonconformance recurs, addressing root cause rather than just the symptom. Issuing rework with only a limited review of recurrence data is exactly the gap that allows the pattern to continue unaddressed. Stopping the recording of future occurrences would hide the pattern rather than correct it. Removing the MRB from repeat nonconformance decisions eliminates the very function needed to recognize and act on the pattern.
  64. The correct answer is B (Acceptable quality level, the maximum percent defective treated as satisfactory): Acceptable quality level, the maximum percent defective treated as satisfactory: AQL is the benchmark process average quality that a sampling plan is designed to accept most of the time. It is a producer-oriented reference point, not a guarantee about any single lot. LTPD instead describes the poor quality level a plan is designed to reject most of the time. AOQL and consumer's risk describe different aspects of sampling plan performance entirely.
  65. The correct answer is C (Producer's risk, tied to the AQL): Producer's risk, tied to the AQL: producer's risk (alpha) is the probability that a sampling plan rejects a lot whose true quality is actually at or better than the AQL. It reflects the supplier's exposure to random sample variation rather than any true process problem. Consumer's risk instead applies at the LTPD, describing the buyer's exposure to accepting a genuinely bad lot. Reducing producer's risk generally requires a larger sample size for a given acceptance number.
  66. The correct answer is A (Consumer's risk, the probability of accepting a lot at the LTPD): consumer's risk (beta) is defined as the probability that a sampling plan will accept a lot whose actual quality is as poor as the LTPD. It represents the protection level the buyer can expect against seriously defective incoming lots. A smaller consumer's risk generally requires a larger sample relative to the acceptance number. Producer's risk describes the opposite exposure, borne by the supplier at the AQL, and acceptable quality level and rectifying inspection describe different aspects of a sampling plan entirely.
  67. The correct answer is C (Probability of acceptance decreases as the true percent defective increases): every valid OC curve slopes downward from left to right because higher incoming defect rates make it statistically less likely that a sample of n = 80 with c = 2 will pass. This downward slope is what allows the plan to discriminate between acceptable and unacceptable lot quality. A flat curve would mean the plan provides no real protection. The plan's ability to distinguish clearly between good and bad lots depends on the steepness of the decline, which is set by the chosen sample size and acceptance number, not on the plan lacking discriminating power altogether.
  68. The correct answer is B (Lot tolerance percent defective): LTPD is the percent defective a sampling plan is designed to reject with high probability, typically corresponding to a consumer's risk of about 10 percent. It sits on the poor-quality end of the operating characteristic curve, opposite the AQL. Sampling plans are often specified by pairing an AQL and producer's risk with an LTPD and consumer's risk. Average outgoing quality limit describes the worst-case post-inspection quality under rectifying sampling, and Type I error probability describes producer's risk, a different concept tied to rejecting a good lot rather than defining the poor-quality rejection point.
  69. The correct answer is A (General inspection level II, the standard default level): General inspection level II, the standard default level: Level II is the default inspection level in ANSI/ASQ Z1.4 and is used unless the contract or quality plan specifies otherwise. Level I gives less discrimination with smaller samples for less critical situations, while Level III gives more discrimination with larger samples. The S-1 through S-4 special levels are reserved for situations such as destructive or costly testing where small samples are preferred. Choosing the wrong level changes the sample size code letter and therefore the entire sampling plan.
  70. The correct answer is D (Switch to tightened inspection until the supplier demonstrates improvement): Z1.4's switching rules require moving from normal to tightened inspection when two out of five or fewer consecutive lots have been rejected on original inspection. Tightened inspection uses a smaller acceptance number for the same sample size, making it harder for marginal lots to pass. The supplier can return to normal inspection only after meeting the standard's reinstatement criteria. Reduced inspection is only appropriate when quality has been consistently good, the opposite situation here.
  71. The correct answer is C (Variables sampling can achieve equivalent protection with a smaller sample): because variables data carries more information per measurement than a simple pass or fail result, Z1.9 plans typically achieve comparable protection against poor quality with a smaller sample size than an equivalent Z1.4 attributes plan. This can reduce inspection time and cost for characteristics that are already measured numerically. The tradeoff is that variables plans do require assuming the measured characteristic follows a specified distribution, usually normal, contrary to the claim that this assumption is unnecessary. Variables plans typically need a smaller sample than an equivalent attributes plan to achieve the same protection, not a larger one, and they still require a defined acceptance limit rather than removing that need.
  72. The correct answer is B (It is not a random sample representative of the whole lot): acceptance sampling decisions are only valid statistically when the sample is drawn so every unit in the lot has a known chance of selection. Always taking the most accessible units can systematically miss defects concentrated elsewhere in the pallet, such as units packed last or units near a process change. This biases the sample and can make a genuinely bad lot appear acceptable. Proper sampling procedures typically require a randomization method tied to unit position or serial number.
  73. The correct answer is A (Wringing the blocks together using a slight rotating pressure): wringing uses molecular adhesion between two extremely flat, clean gage block surfaces to create a near-zero-gap bond when the blocks are pressed and slid together with light rotating pressure. This technique adds negligible dimensional error compared to mechanical fastening or adhesives, which would introduce inconsistent gaps. Properly wrung blocks can support significant weight without separating. Gage blocks must be clean and free of burrs before wringing to avoid damaging the reference surfaces.
  74. The correct answer is D (The larger sample plan produces a steeper, more discriminating curve): The larger sample plan produces a steeper, more discriminating curve: increasing the sample size while keeping the acceptance number proportionally similar generally steepens the OC curve, making the transition from high to low probability of acceptance occur over a narrower range of incoming defect rates. This improved discrimination lets the plan more reliably separate good lots from bad lots. The tradeoff is the added cost and time of inspecting a larger sample. Both producer's and consumer's risk can actually be reduced simultaneously with a well-chosen larger plan, not increased.
  75. The correct answer is B (0.001 inch, the typical readable resolution of a 25-division vernier): 0.001 inch, the typical readable resolution of a 25-division vernier: a standard vernier caliper with a 25-division vernier scale is designed to be read to a resolution of 0.001 inch, matching the requirement in this scenario. Digital and dial calipers can offer similar or slightly finer resolution but rely on different reading mechanisms. A basic steel rule only resolves to about 0.01 inch or coarser, insufficient for this tolerance. Resolution alone does not guarantee accuracy, so the caliper must also be verified through calibration.
  76. The correct answer is C (An outside micrometer with the appropriate measuring range): An outside micrometer with the appropriate measuring range: an outside micrometer is specifically designed to measure external dimensions such as shaft diameters, and standard micrometers can reliably resolve to 0.0001 inch when properly calibrated and used with correct technique. A dial bore gage instead measures internal diameters and would not fit this application. An optical comparator is better suited for profile or contour inspection rather than a simple diameter check. A surface plate and height gage combination measures height from a reference surface, not a shaft's diameter directly.
  77. The correct answer is B (An optical comparator using a magnified overlay chart of the profile): An optical comparator using a magnified overlay chart of the profile: an optical comparator projects a magnified silhouette of the part onto a screen where it can be visually compared against an overlay chart showing the drawing's tolerance zones, radii, and angles simultaneously. This makes it well suited for quick, whole-profile go or no-go style checks on small stamped or formed parts. A CMM can perform similar checks but typically through discrete point measurements rather than a full visual silhouette comparison. Calipers and dial indicators only provide single linear measurements at a time, not a full profile comparison.
  78. The correct answer is D (A CMM can capture many features and complex geometric relationships in one setup): A CMM can capture many features and complex geometric relationships in one setup: a coordinate measuring machine can probe numerous points across multiple features in a single fixturing, then mathematically evaluate datums, true position, and other geometric tolerances that would be difficult or slow to check manually. This makes it especially valuable for complex housings with many interrelated dimensional requirements. Manual gaging with individual tools would require multiple setups and is more prone to fixturing error across features. CMMs still require a regular calibration schedule to maintain measurement accuracy over time.
  79. The correct answer is B (A destructive tensile test performed to failure on the coupon): A destructive tensile test performed to failure on the coupon: ultimate tensile strength and elongation at break can only be directly obtained by pulling a test coupon to fracture, which permanently destroys that specific sample. The results from the tested coupon are then used to represent the properties of the batch or heat lot it came from. Nondestructive methods like ultrasonic testing or dye penetrant inspection can detect flaws but cannot measure these mechanical properties directly. Visual inspection alone provides no quantitative strength or elongation data at all.
  80. The correct answer is C (Liquid dye penetrant inspection of the casting surfaces): Liquid dye penetrant inspection of the casting surfaces: dye penetrant testing applies a liquid that seeps into fine surface-breaking cracks, then uses a developer to draw the dye back out and make the defect visible, all without damaging the part. This makes it well suited for a situation where every casting must remain usable after inspection. Tensile testing, Charpy impact testing, and sectioning are all destructive methods that would consume or damage the parts tested. Dye penetrant inspection is limited to surface-breaking defects and cannot detect flaws below the surface.
  81. The correct answer is C (A probability of acceptance of only about 0.40, since this plan is a weak discriminator at the stated AQL.): computing the operating characteristic curve for n=50, c=1 at p=0.04 gives Pa equal to the probability of 0 or 1 defectives in 50 trials at a 4 percent defect rate, which works out to approximately 0.40. A well-designed sampling plan should accept a lot at the AQL with a high probability, typically 0.90 or more, so this plan's low Pa at the AQL means it exposes the producer to substantial risk of having good lots rejected. Option A is wrong because it describes what a well-designed plan would achieve, not the actual behavior of this specific n=50, c=1 plan. Option B is wrong because the probability of acceptance is far from negligible at the AQL, just lower than desired. Option D is wrong because probability of acceptance decreases, not increases, as the incoming defect rate rises, which is the defining shape of every OC curve.
  82. The correct answer is D (The lot size and the chosen general inspection level): The lot size and the chosen general inspection level: ANSI/ASQ Z1.4's sample size code letter table is entered using the lot or batch size range and the selected inspection level, which together determine a letter such as J or K. That code letter is then used with the specified AQL in a separate table to find the actual sample size and acceptance number. Supplier history and inspector experience are not direct inputs to this specific table lookup. The AQL is applied afterward, not during the code letter determination step itself.
  83. The correct answer is C (Double sampling can reduce average inspection when the first result is clear-cut): Double sampling can reduce average inspection when the first result is clear-cut: a double sampling plan draws a smaller first sample and only requires a second sample when the result falls in an intermediate zone, so clearly good or clearly bad lots can often be decided with less total inspection than a single sampling plan. This can lower average inspection cost over many lots while providing similar overall protection. The first sample in a double plan is typically smaller than an equivalent single sampling plan's sample size, not larger. Both plan types still require defined acceptance and rejection numbers.
  84. The correct answer is A (Switching to reduced inspection with a smaller sample size): Switching to reduced inspection with a smaller sample size: Z1.4 permits a switch from normal to reduced inspection once a supplier has demonstrated a satisfactory switching score along with stable production and no other disqualifying conditions. Reduced inspection uses a smaller sample size, which lowers inspection cost while quality remains consistently good. Tightened inspection is reserved for the opposite situation, where recent lots have shown an increased rejection rate. Discontinuing inspection entirely is not one of the standard's defined switching states.
  85. The correct answer is D (The measured characteristic must reasonably follow a normal distribution): The measured characteristic must reasonably follow a normal distribution: the standard deviation method in Z1.9 calculates an acceptability constant based on the sample mean and standard deviation, and this calculation assumes the underlying measured characteristic is approximately normally distributed. If the true distribution is heavily skewed or otherwise non-normal, the resulting accept or reject decision may not provide the intended level of protection. The method works with continuous measurements rather than attributes-style pass or fail data, which is exactly what Z1.9 is designed to use. Sample sizes under Z1.9 are typically much smaller than 100 units, not required to exceed it.
  86. The correct answer is B (The range method, using the sample range instead of standard deviation): The range method, using the sample range instead of standard deviation: Z1.9 provides a range method specifically for smaller sample sizes, estimating variability from the sample range rather than calculating a full standard deviation, which simplifies the arithmetic for field use. This method trades some statistical efficiency for ease of calculation when only a small number of units are measured. The standard deviation method remains more efficient for larger samples where a full variance calculation is practical. AOQL, rectifying inspection, and skip-lot sampling address entirely different aspects of a sampling program.
  87. The correct answer is C (A reference used with the AQL to find sample size and acceptance number): A reference used with the AQL to find sample size and acceptance number: the sample size code letter itself does not directly specify a sample size, but is instead used together with the chosen AQL in a separate master table to determine both the actual sample size and the acceptance and rejection numbers. The same code letter paired with different AQL values produces different sampling plans. It has no direct meaning as a supplier rating or a percentage of the lot to inspect destructively. Correctly identifying the code letter is a necessary intermediate step before the final plan can be read from the standard.
  88. The correct answer is C (Plan A costs more to inspect but discriminates more sharply between quality levels): Plan A costs more to inspect but discriminates more sharply between quality levels: a larger sample size such as n = 200 generally produces a steeper operating characteristic curve, which more sharply distinguishes between acceptable and unacceptable incoming quality levels, but at the cost of additional units and time needed to inspect each lot. A smaller plan with a similar ratio of c to n will typically have a flatter curve and less discriminating power. The two plans do not actually produce similarly shaped curves, since sample size directly affects curve steepness. Larger samples generally reduce, rather than eliminate, both producer's and consumer's risk simultaneously when well designed.
  89. The correct answer is D (Discriminating power improves, producing a steeper OC curve): Discriminating power improves, producing a steeper OC curve: increasing sample size while holding a similar acceptance ratio generally sharpens the operating characteristic curve, so the transition between high and low probability of acceptance occurs over a narrower range of true defect rates. This improved discrimination allows the plan to more reliably tell good lots from bad lots. The tradeoff is greater inspection time and cost per lot inspected. Sample size is one of the most direct levers a quality engineer has for adjusting a plan's discriminating power.
  90. The correct answer is A (Average outgoing quality limit, the maximum average outgoing defect level): Average outgoing quality limit, the maximum average outgoing defect level: AOQL represents the worst average outgoing quality a customer should expect to receive over the long run under a rectifying inspection program, since very poor incoming lots get screened and corrected while very good lots pass with little intervention. It is calculated across the full range of possible incoming quality levels to find the peak average outgoing defect rate. AQL instead describes an input target for the supplier's process, not an outgoing quality guarantee. LTPD and producer's risk describe different, unrelated aspects of the sampling plan's protection.
  91. The correct answer is B (Rectifying inspection): Rectifying inspection: rectifying inspection describes a program where rejected lots undergo 100 percent screening to find and correct defects before shipment, which improves the average outgoing quality actually received by the customer compared to simply returning the lot untouched. This approach underlies the calculation of the average outgoing quality limit for a given sampling plan. Skip-lot sampling instead reduces inspection frequency on lots from a demonstrated high-quality supplier, an unrelated concept. Tightened and reduced inspection describe adjustments to sample size and acceptance number, not post-rejection screening.
  92. The correct answer is C (AQL sits near the high-acceptance end while LTPD sits near the low-acceptance end): AQL sits near the high-acceptance end while LTPD sits near the low-acceptance end: on the operating characteristic curve, the AQL corresponds to a quality level with a high probability of acceptance, protecting the supplier, while the LTPD corresponds to a much poorer quality level with a low probability of acceptance, protecting the consumer. LTPD is numerically larger than AQL for any meaningful sampling plan since it describes worse quality. Both concepts apply equally to attributes and variables sampling approaches, not exclusively to one or the other. Confusing the two can lead to selecting a sampling plan with far weaker protection than intended.
  93. The correct answer is D (The ratchet stop or friction thimble mechanism): The ratchet stop or friction thimble mechanism: this mechanism slips at a consistent, limited torque once the spindle contacts the part, preventing the inspector from applying excessive and inconsistent hand pressure that would otherwise crush soft materials or distort readings on hard ones. Using the ratchet or friction thimble every time helps standardize measuring force across different operators. The locking lever instead simply holds a reading in place after measurement, and the sleeve markings only display the dimension being read. Anvil hardness affects wear resistance but does not control the applied measuring force.
  94. The correct answer is B (Clamping pressure is deflecting the flexible part during probing): Clamping pressure is deflecting the flexible part during probing: thin-walled or flexible parts can distort under clamping force, so the shape being measured is not the part's true free-state geometry, producing inconsistent results between setups with slightly different clamping. Reviewing and standardizing fixture design and clamping force is a reasonable first step before suspecting the machine itself. Firmware issues and granite base wear are less likely explanations for setup-to-setup variation on a flexible part specifically. Probe tip diameter mainly affects the ability to reach small features, not general part distortion.
  95. The correct answer is C (A c = 0 plan's probability of acceptance drops sharply even at low defect rates): A c = 0 plan's probability of acceptance drops sharply even at low defect rates: because any single nonconforming unit found in the sample causes rejection, zero-acceptance-number plans tend to have operating characteristic curves that fall off quickly even at relatively low true defect levels, making them fairly strict compared to plans allowing one or more defectives. This characteristic can make c = 0 plans attractive for safety-critical components where tight control is desired. No sampling plan can literally guarantee zero defects reach the customer, since sampling always carries some inherent risk. Operating characteristic curves can be constructed for any valid n and c combination, including c = 0.
  96. The correct answer is A (A Rockwell hardness test using a diamond or ball indenter): A Rockwell hardness test using a diamond or ball indenter: Rockwell hardness testing presses a small, calibrated indenter into the part's surface and measures the depth of penetration, leaving only a minor indentation that is generally considered acceptable for most finished parts and allows the part to remain in service. This makes it a practical, near-nondestructive option for routine hardness verification after heat treatment. Tensile testing, Charpy impact testing, and destructive sectioning all consume or permanently damage the test piece and cannot be performed on parts intended to ship. Hardness values from Rockwell testing can also be correlated to approximate tensile strength for some materials.
  97. The correct answer is D (A Charpy impact test performed on a notched specimen): A Charpy impact test performed on a notched specimen: the Charpy test strikes a notched specimen with a calibrated pendulum and measures the energy absorbed during fracture, directly indicating toughness and brittle fracture resistance, especially when performed at a specified low temperature relevant to service conditions. This test destroys the specimen and cannot be repeated on the same piece. Optical comparators, dye penetrant inspection, and CMM measurements evaluate dimensions or surface conditions rather than fracture energy. Charpy results are commonly used to establish a ductile to brittle transition temperature for a given material.
  98. The correct answer is B (Ultrasonic testing, detecting internal flaws through sound wave reflection): Ultrasonic testing, detecting internal flaws through sound wave reflection: ultrasonic testing transmits high-frequency sound waves into the material and analyzes reflections from internal discontinuities such as voids, inclusions, or laminations, making it well suited for inspecting thick plate before welding without damaging the material. Magnetic particle and dye penetrant inspection are both limited to detecting surface or near-surface indications only. Visual inspection under normal lighting cannot reveal any subsurface condition at all. Proper ultrasonic testing requires trained technicians and calibrated reference standards to correctly interpret the signal responses.
  99. The correct answer is C (Radiographic testing using film or a digital detector): Radiographic testing using film or a digital detector: radiographic testing passes penetrating radiation such as X-rays or gamma rays through the weld, and internal discontinuities like porosity, slag, or lack of fusion appear as density variations on film or a digital detector, producing a permanent record of the internal condition. This allows evaluation without cutting into the completed weld. Magnetic particle inspection and eddy current testing are limited to detecting surface or near-surface indications, not deep internal flaws. Visual inspection alone cannot reveal conditions hidden beneath the weld surface.
  100. The correct answer is A (Magnetic particle inspection of the ferromagnetic part): Magnetic particle inspection of the ferromagnetic part: magnetic particle inspection works by magnetizing a ferromagnetic part and applying fine iron particles that concentrate at surface and near-surface discontinuities, revealing cracks as visible particle accumulations under proper lighting. This method requires the part to be made of a material that can be magnetized, which limits its use on nonmagnetic materials like aluminum. A bend test would be a destructive method and is not appropriate when the part must remain usable. Ultrasonic immersion testing is better suited for detecting deeper internal flaws rather than fine surface cracking.
  101. The correct answer is D (It introduces selection bias that can misrepresent the true lot quality): It introduces selection bias that can misrepresent the true lot quality: choosing samples based on convenient physical location rather than a documented random selection method can systematically favor or exclude units produced at a particular time or under particular conditions, undermining the statistical validity of the acceptance decision. A biased sample can make a bad lot look acceptable or an acceptable lot look bad, regardless of the stated sample size. ANSI/ASQ Z1.4 assumes random sampling as a foundational requirement and is not automatically satisfied by convenience sampling. Documented, defensible randomization procedures help protect the integrity of the entire acceptance sampling program.
  102. The correct answer is A (A low probability of acceptance, typically near 0.10 or less): A low probability of acceptance, typically near 0.10 or less: at the LTPD, a well-constructed sampling plan is designed to have a low probability of acceptance, commonly around 0.10, reflecting the consumer's risk associated with that poor quality level. This is the opposite behavior from the AQL, where probability of acceptance is intentionally kept high. A curve showing identical probabilities at both the AQL and LTPD would indicate a plan with essentially no discriminating power. Probability of acceptance always decreases, never increases, as the true defect rate rises on a valid OC curve.
  103. The correct answer is C (Blind sample testing): Blind sample testing: presenting samples to inspectors without identifying information such as supplier or lot origin, often called blind testing, helps reduce the risk that an inspector's prior expectations about a particular source will unconsciously influence subjective judgment calls or borderline measurement decisions. This practice supports the overall integrity of the sampling and inspection process. Skip-lot sampling and reduced inspection instead refer to reducing inspection frequency or sample size for a demonstrated high-quality source. Rectifying inspection refers to 100 percent screening of rejected lots, an unrelated concept.
  104. The correct answer is D (Skip-lot sampling, inspecting only a fraction of submitted lots): Skip-lot sampling, inspecting only a fraction of submitted lots: skip-lot sampling allows a buyer to inspect only some fraction of lots from a supplier that has demonstrated sustained, consistently good quality, reducing overall inspection burden while retaining periodic verification. Eligibility typically requires meeting defined qualification criteria and can be revoked if quality deteriorates. Rectifying inspection and tightened inspection describe different responses used when quality is uncertain or poor, the opposite situation. The Z1.9 range method is a variables sampling calculation technique unrelated to inspection frequency reduction.
  105. The correct answer is B (A running measure of how consistently recent lots have passed inspection): A running measure of how consistently recent lots have passed inspection: the switching score in Z1.4 accumulates points based on the acceptance results of consecutive lots under normal inspection, and reaching the required threshold demonstrates a sustained pattern of good quality before reduced inspection is permitted. This provides an objective, standardized basis for the switching decision rather than relying on subjective judgment alone. Factors such as shipment value, employee count, or shipping distance play no role in this specific calculation. The score resets or is affected by events such as lot rejections or production irregularities.
  106. The correct answer is B (A CMM, since it can probe features across multiple orientations and axes): A CMM, since it can probe features across multiple orientations and axes: a coordinate measuring machine can access and measure features across multiple faces and orientations in three-dimensional space within a single setup, making it well suited for a complex bracket with compound angles and multiple true position requirements. An optical comparator is generally limited to viewing a single silhouette or profile at a time and works best for simpler, largely two-dimensional part geometry. Both tools can, in principle, support true position evaluation, but a CMM handles complex multi-axis features far more directly. CMM inspection does require programming, though it can be reused efficiently for repeat inspections of the same part.
  107. The correct answer is C (Metrological traceability to a national standard): Metrological traceability to a national standard: metrological traceability describes an unbroken chain of calibrations, each with stated measurement uncertainty, that connects a working measurement standard such as a gage block set back to a recognized national or international standard, commonly maintained through an organization like NIST. This traceability provides confidence that measurements made with the gage blocks are accurate and comparable across different labs and locations. Rectifying inspection and skip-lot sampling are acceptance sampling concepts unrelated to calibration traceability. AOQL similarly describes sampling plan performance rather than measurement system traceability.
  108. The correct answer is A (The smaller upper jaws designed for internal measurements): The smaller upper jaws designed for internal measurements: most dial and digital calipers include a second, smaller set of jaws above the main external jaws specifically shaped to reach inside a bore or slot and measure internal dimensions. These internal jaws typically require a small correction to be added to the raw reading to account for their own thickness, which most modern calipers apply automatically. The depth rod instead measures depth from a reference surface, and the main scale and locking screw support reading and holding a measurement rather than enabling internal access. Using the wrong jaw set for an internal measurement can produce a significantly incorrect reading.
  109. The correct answer is D (Whether reliable historical process variability data is already available): Whether reliable historical process variability data is already available: Z1.9 provides both an unknown standard deviation method, which estimates variability from the current sample, and a known standard deviation method, which uses an established process standard deviation from sufficient historical data, and the appropriate choice depends on whether that reliable historical estimate exists. Using the known method when it is truly justified can allow for a smaller sample size while maintaining equivalent protection. The choice is a technical decision based on data availability and process stability, not simply supplier preference. Z1.9 explicitly supports both methods for different practical situations.
  110. The correct answer is B (Because sample size is always smaller than the full lot, leaving inherent risk): Because sample size is always smaller than the full lot, leaving inherent risk: since a sampling plan examines only a portion of the full lot rather than every unit, there is always some statistical chance of accepting a somewhat worse lot or rejecting a somewhat better one, which produces a gradually sloped rather than a sharp vertical operating characteristic curve. Only 100 percent inspection could theoretically approach the ideal step-function curve, and even that is limited by inspection error. AQL and LTPD are deliberately defined as different quality levels, not identical ones, to describe the two ends of this tradeoff. Sampling plans are commonly used for both destructive and nondestructive testing situations, not exclusively destructive testing.
  111. The correct answer is B (Traceability, since it documents the unbroken chain of comparisons back to a national standard): traceability is the documented, unbroken chain of comparisons that links a measurement result back to a recognized national or international standard. Each link in the chain carries its own stated uncertainty, allowing the overall uncertainty of the final measurement to be established. Without this chain, a certificate of calibration has no defensible basis for confirming accuracy. This is why calibration certificates reference the standards used at each step.
  112. The correct answer is C (Bias, since it is a constant offset that does not change with part size): bias is the systematic, constant offset between the average of repeated measurements and the accepted true value, and it does not vary with the magnitude of the value being measured in this scenario. Random error and reproducibility describe scatter or operator-to-operator differences, not a fixed offset. Nonlinearity would describe a bias that changes across the range, which is not what was observed here. Bias is corrected through calibration adjustment or an offset correction factor.
  113. The correct answer is D (The bias changes as the magnitude of the measured value changes): a nonzero slope on a bias-versus-reference-value plot is the defining signature of nonlinearity, meaning the systematic error is not constant across the gage's range. This differs from a simple bias, which would appear as a flat line offset from zero. Operator disagreement and gage discrimination are separate measurement system properties evaluated through different study types. Nonlinearity typically requires recalibration or range-specific correction factors.
  114. The correct answer is C (To detect drift before a device produces unreliable measurement data): calibration intervals exist to periodically verify that a gage still performs within acceptable limits before undetected drift leads to bad accept or reject decisions. Shipping schedules and purchase costs are unrelated to the technical justification for calibration frequency. Operator training remains a separate requirement regardless of calibration status. Proper interval management balances risk of undetected drift against calibration cost and downtime.
  115. The correct answer is B (Environmental sources of measurement error such as temperature humidity and handling contamination): controlling temperature, humidity, and handling of reference gage blocks specifically protects against environmental effects such as thermal expansion and contamination that introduce measurement error. This practice does not address reproducibility, which concerns operator differences, or nonlinearity, which concerns range-dependent bias. Sampling error relates to inspection plan design, not gage storage conditions. Environmental controls are especially critical for reference-grade standards used in calibration.

The correct answer is A (The %R&R is roughly 31.6 percent, which is unacceptable for most applications): the square root of the sum of Repeatability and Reproducibility variance, 2.5 plus 0.5 equals 3.0, divided by the Total variance of 30.0, equals 0.10, and the square root of 0.10 is approximately 0.316, or 31.6 percent. A %R&R above 30 percent is generally considered unacceptable and requires improvement before the system is used for critical decisions. The 8.3 percent figure listed is the percent contribution of Repeatability alone, not the combined %R&R. This distinction between percent contribution and the combined %R&R formula is a common point of confusion.
  117. The correct answer is B (Reproducibility, the variation caused by different operators measuring the same parts): reproducibility specifically measures the variation that arises when different operators, or different conditions such as different setups, measure the same parts with the same gage. Repeatability instead measures variation when a single operator repeats measurements under identical conditions. Stability and bias describe drift over time and systematic offset, which are unrelated to operator-to-operator differences. High reproducibility variation often points to a need for standardized measurement procedures or operator training.
  118. The correct answer is D (Repeatability, the variation from one operator repeating the same measurement under identical setup): repeatability is defined as the variation observed when the same operator measures the same part multiple times using the same instrument under identical setup conditions. This isolates the equipment and short-term measurement variation from any operator-to-operator effect. Reproducibility would instead involve different operators, and linearity and accuracy address entirely different measurement system characteristics. High repeatability variation often points toward equipment wear, looseness, or fixturing problems.
  119. The correct answer is D (Stability, the change in a gage's bias over an extended period of time): stability refers to the change in a gage's bias over time when measuring the same reference standard under the same conditions, and a gradual upward shift in average reading while spread remains constant is the classic signature of a stability loss. Precision concerns the spread of readings, which the scenario states remained constant, so precision was not lost here. Discrimination and resolution describe the smallest detectable change, not drift over time. Stability is typically monitored using a control chart on a fixed reference standard.
  120. The correct answer is A (Measurement correlation, the comparison of two different gages measuring identical parts): measurement correlation is the practice of comparing results from two different measurement devices on the same parts to confirm they produce equivalent, consistent readings before one replaces or supplements the other. Attribute agreement analysis instead applies to pass or fail judgment consistency, not two variable gages being compared. Destructive gage R&R and calibration interval extension are unrelated concepts to this comparison activity. Measurement correlation studies help ensure a smooth transition between old and new inspection equipment.
  121. Accuracy instead addresses closeness to the true value, and bias is the systematic offset component of accuracy. Traceability concerns the documented chain linking measurements to a national standard, not the spread of repeated readings. A gage can be highly precise yet still inaccurate if it is consistently biased away from the truth.
  122. The correct answer is C (Accuracy describes closeness to the true value while precision describes the spread among repeated readings): this is the standard distinction used in measurement system analysis, where accuracy addresses correctness and precision addresses consistency. The two terms are not interchangeable, since a gage can be precise without being accurate or accurate without being precise. This distinction applies to both attribute and variable gages, not exclusively to one type. Recognizing this difference is essential before interpreting any capability or bias study.
  123. It says nothing about the country of manufacture of the gage itself. Traceability does not exempt a device from future periodic recalibration. It also does not eliminate the need for internal calibration audits within the receiving organization.

Resolution is also distinct from reproducibility, which concerns operator-to-operator variation rather than the gage's inherent display sensitivity. Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Process decision program chart): Process decision program chart: A PDPC is built specifically to map out a plan's sequential steps alongside potential problems at each step and the countermeasures needed to address them, making it ideal for risk anticipation before execution. An activity network diagram sequences tasks by duration, an affinity diagram groups ideas, and an interrelationship digraph maps cause-and-effect among issues, none of which build in contingency planning.
  2. The correct answer is D (Matrix diagram): Matrix diagram: A matrix diagram displays two or more sets of items, such as customer requirements and design features, in a grid format and shows the strength of the relationship at each intersection. A tree diagram decomposes a single goal, an affinity diagram groups unstructured ideas, and force field analysis maps opposing forces, none of which compare two distinct lists against each other.
  3. The correct answer is A (Interrelationship digraph): Interrelationship digraph: An interrelationship digraph is designed to map cause-and-effect connections among a set of complex, interconnected issues, revealing which issues act as root drivers and which are downstream effects. A check sheet tallies simple counts, a histogram shows a distribution shape, and a prioritization matrix ranks options against criteria, none of which map relationships between interconnected problems.
  4. The correct answer is C (Prioritization matrix): Prioritization matrix: A prioritization matrix is built specifically to rank a list of options against multiple weighted criteria, producing an objective basis for selecting which projects to pursue. A tree diagram decomposes a goal, a PDPC anticipates problems in an existing plan, and an affinity diagram groups raw ideas, none of which score options against weighted criteria.
  5. The correct answer is B (Activity network diagram): Activity network diagram: An activity network diagram sequences project tasks, shows their durations and dependencies, and identifies the critical path that determines the shortest overall completion time. A tree diagram decomposes goals into tasks without timing, a matrix diagram compares lists, and force field analysis maps opposing forces, none of which model task sequencing and duration.
  6. The correct answer is A (The 80/20 principle, meaning a few categories drive most of the problems): The 80/20 principle, meaning a few categories drive most of the problems: This pattern, where a small number of categories account for the large majority of occurrences, is the Pareto principle in action and directs improvement resources toward the vital few categories. The central limit theorem concerns sampling distributions, the law of large numbers concerns sample size and variation, and Occam's razor is a general reasoning heuristic, none of which describe this concentration pattern.
  7. The correct answer is C (Solder bridge, Cold joint, Missing component): Solder bridge, Cold joint, Missing component: These three categories total 40 plus 24 plus 16, equaling 80 of the 100 total defects, matching the classic 80 percent threshold used in Pareto analysis. Focusing corrective action on these three categories addresses the large majority of solder defects while leaving the remaining, lower-impact categories for later attention.
  8. The correct answer is D (Environment): Environment: Ambient temperature swings in the shop are an external condition affecting the workpiece, which places this cause squarely under the Environment category rather than Method, Machine, or Material. Environment captures surrounding conditions such as temperature, humidity, lighting, and noise that influence process outcomes without being part of the procedure, equipment, or input material itself.

A top-down flowchart shows only major steps without ownership, a block diagram is a simplified high-level view, and a data flow diagram tracks information movement rather than responsibility, none of which highlight cross-functional handoffs as clearly.

An X-bar and R chart is used for continuous variable data, a c chart requires constant sample size for counting defects, and an individuals chart tracks single continuous measurements, none of which fit variable-size attribute data.

Cost reduction, certification, and standardization are useful tools, but TQM treats them as means to satisfy the customer, not ends in themselves. When customer focus is lost, technically compliant processes can still produce products nobody wants. This customer-first orientation is why TQM links quality directly to long-term organizational survival.

This contrasts with Kaikaku, which describes a radical, large-scale redesign. Capital projects and executive strategic planning are legitimate activities, but they are not what defines the Kaizen philosophy. The power of Kaizen comes from sustained small changes accumulating into large gains over time.

Cleaning without inspection wastes an opportunity to catch developing problems early, and inspection limited to scheduled maintenance misses issues that develop between those intervals. This combined approach keeps the total number of 5S pillars unchanged; it simply makes each cleaning cycle more valuable. Regular Shine activity often surfaces maintenance needs long before a formal inspection would.

Without Sustain, even a well-executed 5S launch typically reverts to its previous disorganized state within months. Designing the ideal layout belongs to the future state map, which is built only after the current state is understood, not before any data is collected. A clear current state map is the foundation every future state improvement is built on.

Simply re-mapping the current state again would not move the team toward improvement. A red-tag list belongs to the 5S Sort step, and a control chart is a statistical monitoring tool, neither of which redesigns the value stream flow. The future state map, paired with an implementation plan, is what converts value stream analysis into real process change.

  59. The correct answer is D (Pulling material based on actual downstream demand signals): A Kanban system is a visual pull mechanism where production or replenishment is triggered only when a downstream process actually consumes material, preventing overproduction. Producing to a forecast and centralized push scheduling are hallmarks of traditional push systems, which Kanban is specifically designed to replace. Batching to reduce changeovers is a separate lean tactic that can coexist with either push or pull, but it does not define Kanban itself. Pull-based replenishment keeps inventory levels aligned with real consumption rather than projected demand.
  60. The correct answer is D (A two-bin Kanban system): The two-bin method is one of the simplest forms of Kanban, using the emptying of one bin as the visual trigger to replenish it while production continues uninterrupted from the second bin. SMED addresses reducing changeover time on equipment and has no relationship to bin-based replenishment. A value stream map is a flow diagram, not a replenishment mechanism, and a control chart is a statistical monitoring tool. The two-bin system requires no computer or paperwork, making it popular for high-volume, low-cost components.
  61. The correct answer is B (To make abnormal conditions visible immediately so they can be addressed): An andon system is a visual control tool designed to surface problems the moment they occur so that support can respond before a small issue becomes a larger quality or delivery failure. It is not intended to trigger a full plant shutdown, and it does not calculate OEE, which is a separate performance metric derived from availability, performance, and quality data. Standardized work instructions remain necessary at each station regardless of whether andon is in use. Rapid visibility and response are what distinguish andon from a passive reporting system.

  63. The correct answer is A (Overproduction, making more than what is currently needed): Producing a full month's supply in one batch, ahead of actual customer demand, is a textbook example of overproduction, often considered the most damaging of the eight wastes because it generates most of the others, including excess inventory and additional handling. Waiting refers to idle time when work cannot proceed, Transportation refers to unnecessary movement of material between locations, and Motion refers to unnecessary physical movement by people. High changeover time is a real constraint, but the correct long-term fix is a SMED effort to reduce it, not building excess inventory to avoid changeovers.
  64. The correct answer is B (Waiting, idle time caused by an unbalanced or delayed process): Waiting occurs whenever people, material, or equipment sit idle because a preceding step in the process has not delivered what is needed on time, exactly as described at this workstation. Overprocessing refers to doing more work than the customer values, Inventory refers to holding excess stock, and Defects refers to nonconforming output. Balancing line cycle times, often through standardized work and takt time analysis, is the typical corrective action for chronic waiting waste. Reducing waiting improves both throughput and morale, since idle operators often perceive the delay as a management failure to plan capacity.
  65.   66. The correct answer is D (Transportation, unnecessary movement of material between locations): Transportation waste specifically refers to unnecessary movement of materials, parts, or products between locations, as illustrated by subassemblies traveling across the building multiple times with no value added during the moves. Motion refers to unnecessary movement of people, not material, while performing their actual work. Overprocessing refers to performing steps beyond what the customer requires, and Inventory refers to holding excess stock rather than moving it. A revised facility layout that groups sequential process steps closer together is the typical countermeasure for reducing transportation waste.
  67. The correct answer is C (Overprocessing, performing work beyond what the customer values): Overprocessing occurs when a process step, such as an inspection for a dimension no longer required by the customer, continues to be performed even though it adds no value the customer is willing to pay for. Defects refers to nonconforming output itself, not an unnecessary inspection step, Waiting refers to idle time from process imbalance, and Motion refers to unnecessary physical movement while doing value-added work. Reviewing work instructions after every engineering change is a practical way to catch and eliminate overprocessing before it becomes a standing waste.
  68. The correct answer is C (Inventory, holding more material than current demand requires): Carrying six months of raw material when the supplier lead time is only two weeks ties up capital, consumes storage space, and hides other problems such as quality issues or demand variability, all classic symptoms of excess inventory waste. Waiting and Motion describe idle time and unnecessary movement respectively, neither of which matches holding excess raw stock. Overproduction refers to producing finished goods ahead of demand, which is a related but distinct waste from holding raw material. Reducing lot sizes and improving supplier reliability are common ways to bring inventory levels closer to actual consumption.
  69. The correct answer is D (83 percent): Dimension Out of Tolerance, Surface Finish Defect, and Incorrect Material Cert together account for 83 of the 100 rejections shown, 35 plus 28 plus 20. This is the classic Pareto insight that a small number of causes typically drives most of the effect.
  70. The correct answer is B (Motion, unnecessary movement of people while performing work): Motion waste describes unnecessary physical movement by the person doing the work, such as bending, reaching, or walking, which adds time and fatigue without adding value. Transportation instead refers to unnecessary movement of material or product, not the operator's own body. Overprocessing refers to extra work steps beyond customer requirements, and Inventory refers to excess stock levels rather than workstation ergonomics. Relocating the fastener bin within easy reach at the front of the bench is a simple example of eliminating motion waste through better workstation layout.
  71. The correct answer is A (Defects, producing output that fails to meet requirements): Defects waste refers to any output that fails to conform to specifications, whether it must be scrapped, as in this sealed-housing example, or reworked at additional cost. Waiting describes idle time from process delays, Motion describes unnecessary physical movement, and Overproduction describes producing beyond current demand, none of which describes nonconforming output itself. Poka-yoke devices that prevent assembly from continuing when a fastener is missing are a common countermeasure used specifically to eliminate this type of defect at the source.
  72.
