Man addresses staffing and training, Machine addresses bearing wear and gauge accuracy, and Environment addresses ambient conditions around the equipment. Recognizing this as a Method gap directs the fix toward creating and documenting a formal preventive maintenance program rather than simply repairing the current breakdown. Without addressing the Method root cause, the same type of unplanned failure is likely to recur on this or similar assets.
  73. The correct answer is B (Underutilized talent, failing to use employees' knowledge and ideas): The eighth waste, often added to the original seven wastes to form the acronym TIMWOODS, refers to failing to engage employees' skills, knowledge, and improvement ideas, exactly as described when an operator's fixture idea is never solicited or captured. Overproduction, Transportation, and Inventory each describe a different type of physical waste in the flow of material, not a failure to use human potential. Suggestion systems, Kaizen events, and Gemba walks are common structures used specifically to capture and act on frontline improvement ideas before they are lost. Underutilized talent is often considered one of the most costly wastes because it also suppresses future improvement.
  74. The correct answer is D (Together they define pace, method, and minimum inventory to run smoothly): Standardized work requires takt time to set the required pace, the work sequence to define the repeatable method, and standard work-in-process to specify the minimum inventory needed to keep the operation running without interruption. Any single element alone leaves gaps, since knowing the pace without the method or the buffer needed to sustain it does not fully define how the work should be performed. This is why standardized work documentation always presents all three elements together on a combination sheet. Leaving one element out is a common audit finding when standardized work is only partially implemented.
  75. The correct answer is C (3.0 minutes per unit): Available production time is 450 minutes minus the 30 minutes reserved for breaks and cleanup, leaving 420 minutes of actual working time. Dividing 420 minutes by the required 140 units gives a takt time of exactly 3.0 minutes per unit. Takt time represents the pace at which one unit must be completed to keep exactly in step with customer demand, not the actual time an operator takes to perform the work. If the measured cycle time exceeds this 3.0 minute takt time, the cell will fall behind and fail to meet the shift's demand.
  76. The correct answer is D (The cell will fall behind demand until the bottleneck cycle time is reduced): When the bottleneck station's cycle time of 3.1 minutes exceeds the takt time of 2.5 minutes, the cell cannot produce units fast enough to keep pace with customer demand, and output will progressively fall behind over the shift. The cell will not exceed demand under this condition, and the mismatch between takt time and cycle time is a real and actionable problem, not something to disregard. Slowing the bottleneck further would make the shortfall worse rather than better. Line balancing, work redistribution, or a targeted improvement project is needed to bring the bottleneck's cycle time at or below takt time.
  77. The correct answer is C (External setup, since they can be performed while the machine runs): SMED classifies setup tasks that can be prepared or performed while the equipment is still producing the current job as external setup, such as retrieving and pre-heating the next die. Internal setup refers to the opposite category, tasks that genuinely require the machine to be stopped, like removing the current die and mounting the new one. Standardized work elements and Kanban replenishment are separate lean tools unrelated to this internal-versus-external distinction. The core SMED strategy is to convert as many internal tasks as possible into external ones, since only internal time actually stops production.
  78. The correct answer is A (Reduce equipment changeover time to enable smaller batch sizes): SMED's core purpose is to dramatically shorten changeover time so that production can economically run smaller batches, which in turn reduces inventory and improves flexibility to respond to customer demand. It does not eliminate the need for preventive maintenance, which addresses a completely different aspect of equipment reliability. Adding more operators to a changeover is one possible tactic within a SMED event, but it is not the underlying goal itself. A documented standardized work sheet for the changeover procedure is actually a typical output of a successful SMED effort, not something it replaces.
  79. The correct answer is B (The distribution is roughly centered but has parts exceeding the USL): The histogram shows a single peak near the 10.00-10.05 bin with counts tapering off on both sides, indicating a roughly centered, unimodal distribution rather than two separate streams or a heavily skewed shape. However, the bin above the USL of 10.10 still contains 4 parts, showing that some output exceeds the upper specification limit despite the distribution's generally centered shape. This combination suggests the process has adequate centering but excessive spread relative to the tolerance band. Reducing variation, rather than simply re-centering the process, would be the appropriate next step to eliminate the out-of-spec parts.
  80. The correct answer is B (Unplanned downtime and changeover time that stopped the equipment): Availability measures the proportion of scheduled production time that the equipment was actually able to run, so it is reduced specifically by events that stop the equipment entirely, such as breakdowns and changeovers. Slow cycles and minor stops that do not fully halt production instead reduce the Performance component of OEE, not Availability. Scrap and rework reduce the Quality component, since the equipment was still running when the defective output was produced. Correctly separating these three loss categories is essential because each points to a different type of corrective action.
  81. The correct answer is C (Performance, since it measures actual speed against ideal cycle time): Performance specifically captures speed losses, including running slower than the rated ideal cycle time and accumulated minor stops that do not count as full downtime, exactly as described for this filling line. Availability instead measures whether the equipment ran during its scheduled time at all, which is not the issue here since downtime was minimal. Quality measures the proportion of good units versus total units produced, which is a separate loss category from running speed. Overall Equipment Effectiveness is the combined metric derived from all three components, not the individual component responsible for this specific speed-related loss.
  82. The correct answer is A (Quality, since it measures good units against total units produced): The Quality component of OEE is calculated as good units divided by total units produced, so the 200 scrapped units directly reduce this ratio even though the equipment ran at full speed with minimal downtime. Availability and Performance are unaffected in this scenario, since neither downtime nor speed was the source of the loss described. Takt time is an entirely separate concept describing the pace needed to match customer demand, not a component of OEE. Distinguishing which OEE component a loss belongs to ensures the improvement effort targets the actual cause, in this case a quality problem rather than a speed or uptime problem.
  83. The correct answer is D (73 percent): OEE is calculated by multiplying Availability, Performance, and Quality together, so 0.90 multiplied by 0.85 multiplied by 0.95 equals approximately 0.7268, or about 73 percent when rounded to the nearest whole percent. Simply averaging the three percentages or reporting the highest individual value would overstate true equipment effectiveness, since OEE is intentionally multiplicative to reflect how losses compound across all three categories. A world-class OEE benchmark is often cited as around 85 percent, so this result of 73 percent indicates meaningful room for improvement. Breaking the result back down into its three components tells the team exactly where to focus that improvement effort.
  84. The correct answer is C (Performance, since both losses reduce output relative to rated speed): Within the Six Big Losses framework, reduced speed and idling and minor stops are both classified under Performance, because each causes the equipment to produce less output than its ideal cycle time would predict, without necessarily stopping it entirely or generating defects. Availability instead covers breakdowns and setup and adjustment losses that fully halt production. Quality covers process defects and reduced yield losses that occur while the equipment is otherwise running. Recognizing that these two losses belong to Performance, not Availability, keeps improvement efforts focused on speed and micro-stoppage causes rather than searching for a downtime event that never happened.
  85. The correct answer is B (DMAIC applies a more rigorous, data-intensive structure to complex projects): While both PDCA and DMAIC are cyclical improvement frameworks built on similar logic, DMAIC typically applies a more formal, statistically rigorous structure with defined tools at each phase, making it well suited to complex Six Sigma projects with significant variation to analyze. PDCA is often used for simpler, faster improvement cycles embedded in daily work and Kaizen activity. The two frameworks are commonly used side by side within the same organization for different types of problems, not as mutually exclusive choices. DMAIC does include a Control phase, which serves a very similar purpose to PDCA's Act phase in locking in the improvement.
  86. The correct answer is D (A focused, time-bound event that implements rapid, visible changes): A structured Kaizen event, sometimes called a Kaizen blitz, brings a dedicated cross-functional team together for a short, intensive period, typically three to five days, to analyze a specific problem and implement visible changes before the event ends. This differs from an individual suggestion submitted informally, which represents a much smaller and less structured form of continuous improvement. It is also distinct from a multi-year strategic initiative, since a Kaizen event is deliberately narrow in scope and fast in execution. While outside facilitators sometimes support a Kaizen event, the work is performed by the cross-functional team itself, not delegated entirely to a consultant.
  87. The correct answer is A (A Gemba walk, observing the real workplace where value is created): Gemba is a Japanese term meaning the actual place, and a Gemba walk refers specifically to leaders going to the shop floor to observe real work, real problems, and real data firsthand rather than relying solely on secondhand reports. A DMAIC control phase audit is a distinct project-closing activity focused on sustaining a specific improvement's gains. A value stream mapping session conducted only in a conference room, without direct floor observation, misses the core purpose of Gemba, which is grounding decisions in direct observation. A Kanban replenishment review is a narrower materials management activity, not a general leadership practice for understanding the work itself.
  88. The correct answer is A (A poka-yoke, a mistake-proofing device that prevents the error): A poka-yoke is a mistake-proofing mechanism specifically designed to make an error physically impossible or immediately detectable, such as a fixture that will not allow assembly to proceed unless every component is correctly seated. A Kanban card is a replenishment signal unrelated to preventing assembly errors, and a value stream map is a flow diagram used for analysis rather than error prevention. A control chart monitors statistical variation over time but does not physically prevent a specific assembly mistake from occurring. Poka-yoke devices are frequently the outcome of a Kaizen event that targets a recurring, well-understood defect at its source.
  89. A single normal distribution produces one central peak, not two separated ones, so this shape rules out that explanation. Measurement error alone is an unlikely explanation for such a large and structured separation between two peaks. Because two sources appear to be mixed, the team should indeed separate the data by shift before drawing further conclusions, the opposite of the incorrect option presented.
  90. The correct answer is C (Heijunka, production leveling by volume and product mix): Heijunka refers specifically to leveling both the volume and the mix of production so that a smaller, repeating sequence of products is made each day rather than large batches of a single product run infrequently. Kanban is the pull signal that can support a leveled schedule but does not itself define the leveling concept. SMED reduces the changeover time that makes frequent small-batch, mixed production practical, but it is a distinct enabling technique rather than the leveling concept itself. Poka-yoke is an error-proofing device unrelated to production scheduling. Heijunka helps smooth demand on upstream suppliers and reduces the inventory swings caused by large, infrequent batch runs.
  91. The correct answer is B (Kaizen teian, a formal individual employee suggestion system): Kaizen teian refers specifically to a formal, structured suggestion system that encourages individual employees to submit improvement ideas, provides timely feedback, and recognizes implemented suggestions, often generating a very high volume of small improvements annually. A DMAIC control plan is a project-specific document for sustaining one particular improvement, not an organization-wide suggestion program. A value stream mapping workshop is a single analytical event rather than an ongoing employee engagement system. An OEE scorecard is a performance metric review, unrelated to capturing individual improvement ideas. Kaizen teian is often credited as a major driver of the cumulative improvement culture associated with well-known lean manufacturers.
  92. The correct answer is D (The symbols reveal where excess inventory is likely building up in the flow): Push arrows on a value stream map indicate material moving forward based on schedule rather than actual downstream need, which is precisely the condition that tends to create excess work-in-process inventory between process steps. Pull symbols indicate that material only moves in response to real consumption, which naturally limits inventory buildup at that connection point. The two symbols are not interchangeable and carry distinct analytical meaning about how material actually flows through the value stream. A future state map deliberately seeks to convert push connections into pull connections wherever practical, precisely to reduce the excess inventory that push connections tend to generate.
  93. The correct answer is A (To provide objective feedback that helps sustain the improvements): Recurring, scored 5S audits give area teams objective, visible feedback on whether the organization, cleanliness, and standards established during the launch are still being maintained, which directly supports the Sustain pillar of 5S. Identifying new value stream mapping opportunities, calculating OEE, and determining Six Sigma certification eligibility are all separate activities unrelated to the specific purpose of a 5S audit. Without ongoing audits and visible accountability, 5S gains commonly erode within months as daily production pressures take priority. Posting scores publicly also creates a mild competitive and social incentive that reinforces the habits Sustain depends on.
  94. The correct answer is B (A standardized work combination sheet for the workstation): A standardized work combination sheet lists each work element in sequence with its manual, walking, and automatic time components, giving a visual picture of exactly how an operator's cycle fits within the required takt time. A value stream map instead shows flow and inventory across an entire process, not the detailed time breakdown at a single workstation. A control chart tracks statistical variation in a measured characteristic over time, and a fishbone diagram organizes potential causes of a specific problem, neither of which documents work element timing. The combination sheet is one of the core documents used to both train operators and identify further balancing opportunities at a specific station.
  95. The correct answer is C (Control charts, updated standard operating procedures, and a response plan): The Control phase of DMAIC is specifically focused on sustaining the gains achieved during Improve, which typically requires ongoing statistical process control through control charts, updated documentation such as standard operating procedures, and a defined response plan for when the process signals an out-of-control condition. A fishbone diagram and five-why analysis belong to the Analyze phase, where root causes are still being identified. A project charter and problem statement are Define phase deliverables produced at the very start of the project. A future state value stream map is a separate Lean tool used during flow redesign, not a standard DMAIC Control phase output. Without these Control phase tools in place, process performance commonly drifts back toward its original, unimproved state.
  96. The correct answer is B (Continuous improvement, treating quality as a never-ending pursuit): Continuous improvement is a foundational TQM principle holding that quality is never a completed state, but an ongoing commitment to incremental progress regardless of current performance levels. Statistical process control is a specific measurement and monitoring tool used in support of quality efforts, not the overarching philosophy itself. Just-in-time production is a separate Lean manufacturing strategy focused on inventory and flow, and benchmarking is a comparative technique used periodically rather than continuously. Embedding continuous improvement as a core value, rather than a one-time initiative, is what distinguishes a mature TQM culture from an organization that treats quality as a checklist to complete once.

The correct answer is D (Fatigue crack initiation at a stress concentration followed by progressive crack growth.): beach marks, also called striations at a macro level, are a classic visual signature of fatigue failure, showing the progressive advance of a crack over repeated loading cycles. Their origin at a surface nick near a fillet radius further supports fatigue, since stress concentrations at geometric transitions and surface flaws are common fatigue initiation sites. A sudden overload fracture typically shows a rougher, more chaotic fracture surface without the layered beach mark pattern. Uniform corrosion thinning and creep deformation produce different surface characteristics entirely, not radiating beach marks from a localized origin.

This approach removes reliance on opinion or seniority and lets the data determine the true cause. Deferring to the most senior member, averaging opinions, or choosing the cheapest fix to implement all bypass evidence-based root cause analysis and risk selecting an incorrect or incomplete corrective action.

  1.   2. Not all 45 measurements fall inside the limits, since the two outer bins sit outside them, so the process is not fully capable as shown.   3. The correct answer is B (Independent events): Independent events are defined as events where the outcome of one has no effect on the probability of the other, exactly as described for these two inspection stations. Mutually exclusive events cannot both occur at the same time, which is a different relationship than having no effect on each other. Complementary events are pairs of outcomes that together account for all possibilities, such as pass and fail. Joint events simply refer to two events occurring together and do not by themselves imply independence.
  4. The correct answer is D (Mutually exclusive events.): Because both cannot occur simultaneously. Events that cannot happen at the same time, such as a single part simultaneously passing and failing inspection, are called mutually exclusive events. Independence describes whether one event affects the probability of another, which is a separate concept from whether both can occur together. Conditional events involve the probability of one event given that another has already occurred, not simply an either-or outcome. Joint events describe two events occurring together, which is the opposite of what is happening here since pass and fail cannot occur together.
  5. The correct answer is A (93.1%): For independent events, the probability that both occur is the product of their individual probabilities, so 0.95 multiplied by 0.98 equals 0.931, or 93.1%. 96.5% would result from simply averaging the two probabilities rather than multiplying them. 98.0% only reflects the higher of the two individual probabilities. 100.0% would only be correct if both steps were certain to detect the defect, which is not the case here.
  6. The correct answer is C (97%): Complementary probability states that the probability of an event not occurring equals one minus the probability that it does occur, so 100% minus 3% leaves 97%. 3% is the probability of the defect occurring, not the complementary probability of a good unit. 33% does not result from subtracting 3% from 100%. 100% would only be correct if defects never occurred at all, which contradicts the given 3% defect rate.
  7. A long right tail would pull the mean above the median, but this histogram has no such skew. A long left tail would pull the mean below the median, which also does not match this symmetric shape. Grouped frequency data are sufficient to estimate and compare the mean and median using midpoints and cumulative counts, so individual raw values are not required.

  10. The correct answer is D (Systematic sampling.): Because measurements are taken at fixed, regular intervals. Taking a measurement at a fixed interval, such as every 15 minutes, is the defining feature of systematic sampling. Judgment sampling relies on a person's subjective choice of which units to select, not a fixed time interval. A census would require measuring every single part produced, not one measurement every 15 minutes. Stratified sampling divides the population into subgroups such as shifts and samples within each, which is not described here.
  11. The population of parts being produced is unrelated to whether the measuring instrument itself is in calibration. A single stable process usually produces a single peak, not two separated clusters, so that does not match this shape. The central limit theorem applies to the distribution of sample means across repeated samples, not to the shape of one raw data histogram.   13. The correct answer is A (Nominal scale.): Because the numbers are labels rather than quantities. Even though lot numbers are numeric, they function only as identifying labels with no quantitative meaning, which makes lot number a nominal-scale variable. An interval scale requires equal and meaningful spacing between values, which a lot number sequence does not guarantee. Lot numbers are assigned sequentially as batches are produced and do not indicate quality ranking, so a later lot number does not imply an improved or better lot, ruling out an ordinal interpretation.

This data set is measured on a ratio scale with a true zero, and outlier sensitivity does not change the underlying measurement scale.

The range can be calculated for continuous data just as easily as for discrete data. Calculating the range only requires the maximum and minimum observed values, not any advance knowledge of the population mean. The range and the standard deviation are computed using entirely different formulas, so describing the range as simply a relabeled version of the standard deviation misstates how it is calculated.

This transformed data can then be analyzed using standard normal-based tools such as confidence intervals or control limits. Squaring, taking a reciprocal, or simply subtracting the mean does not correct the underlying skewness the way a logarithmic transform does.

A shape parameter equal to 1 would instead indicate a constant failure rate, and a shape parameter less than 1 would indicate a decreasing failure rate typical of infant mortality. The normal, lognormal, and uniform distributions each have fundamentally different shapes and do not reduce to a Weibull distribution under this condition.

The chi-square distribution models a single variance relative to a known value, not a ratio of two variances. The t distribution and Poisson distribution serve entirely different purposes, comparing means and modeling rare-event counts respectively.

The Poisson distribution instead counts occurrences of a single type of rare event over a fixed interval.

A Type II error would instead involve failing to reject a null hypothesis that is actually false, the opposite mistake. Because a genuine error in decision-making occurred, this cannot be described as a correct decision or as an unclassified sampling error.

However, this change typically reduces the test's power to detect a true effect unless the sample size is also increased, so option C describing an automatic increase in power is incorrect.   75. The correct answer is A (increasing the sample size used in the hypothesis test): a larger sample size reduces the standard error of the estimate, which makes it easier to detect a true shift in the process mean and therefore increases statistical power. Decreasing sample size has the opposite effect, reducing power. Making the significance level more stringent by lowering alpha, with nothing else changed, actually reduces power rather than increasing it, and switching from an appropriate one-tailed test to a two-tailed test similarly reduces the test's sensitivity in the direction of interest.

A two-tailed test would instead be used if the engineer wanted to detect a difference in either direction without a prior directional claim. A paired t-test and a chi-square test of independence are designed for entirely different comparison structures, not for comparing variability between two independent suppliers.

An independent two-sample t-test would incorrectly treat the two sets of measurements as coming from unrelated groups, discarding the pairing information. A one-way ANOVA and a chi-square goodness-of-fit test are built for entirely different comparison structures and do not fit this paired-measurement scenario.

R-squared does not describe how many points fall exactly on the line, since most real datasets show scatter around the fitted line even with a high R-squared value. R-squared is also not the same number as the correlation coefficient, which would be the square root of 0.81, and it does not by itself establish a causal probability.

A correlation coefficient, no matter how strong, never by itself proves that one variable causes changes in the other.

Even a very high correlation coefficient, such as 0.90, does not overcome this fundamental limitation of correlational data. It does not eliminate process variation itself, since the moving average is only a data analysis and presentation technique, not a change to the actual manufacturing process. It also does not transform the data into a normal distribution or calculate exact future probabilities, which would require an entirely different statistical approach.

This distinction determines whether the response should be to leave the process alone or to investigate a specific cause. SPC does not guarantee perfect conformance, and it complements rather than replaces inspection and capability studies.

  96.   98. This is why X-bar/s charts are recommended for larger subgroup sizes such as 12. The R chart remains simpler and is preferred mainly for small subgroups.
  99. Mixing output from three machines into one subgroup blends those between-machine differences into the within-subgroup estimate, hiding a real signal. Subgroups should instead be formed from a single stream, such as one machine over a short time window.

  101. The correct answer is A (Control charts detect process shifts early, preventing defects before they accumulate.): SPC allows a process to be monitored continuously so that a shift toward an out-of-control condition is detected and corrected before large quantities of nonconforming product are made. Final inspection only sorts good from bad after the fact and does nothing to prevent the defects. SPC and inspection serve different, complementary purposes rather than one fully replacing the other.

It should not simply be dismissed or erased from the record. Concluding the limits themselves are wrong ignores the more likely explanation tied directly to the material change.

It remains a variables chart appropriate for continuous data on a low-volume process. p and c charts are attribute charts and are not designed for this purpose.

This is not the kind of behavior expected from ordinary common-cause variation on a stable process. Nothing about this pattern suggests a calculation error in the moving range itself.
  107. A single subgroup at the start of the shift would miss any change that occurs later. Sampling only rejected parts biases the data and defeats the purpose of monitoring the ongoing process.

It should be investigated as a special cause. The rule applies to any control chart, not just X-bar charts, wherever a centerline run is evaluated.
  109. It does not control supplier material variation directly, and it cannot promise a specific equipment-effectiveness gain or eliminate training needs. Realistic SPC benefits center on early detection and reduced cost of poor quality.

Waiting for a point beyond the outer limit would delay detection of a real change. Recalibrating the gauge or redrawing limits from only three points is not the appropriate response to this signal.

Dismissing the timing as unrelated common-cause noise would miss an identifiable root cause. Control limits should reflect a stable process, not be adjusted simply to conceal a real shift.

Measurement convenience or drawing order are not reliable prioritization criteria. Waiting for failures before charting defeats the preventive purpose of SPC.

This rule applies to variables charts such as R charts as well as X-bar charts. It does not require discarding prior data or waiting for more points before acting.

Rational subgroups are meant to represent a narrow snapshot in time so that between-subgroup variation carries the signal. The subgroup size of five is not itself the problem here.
  116. An np chart requires a constant sample size, and a c chart is for defect counts rather than nonconforming units. This data is a count of nonconforming units, not a continuous measurement, so an X-bar chart does not apply.

Adjusting after every part, known as tampering, actually increases variation rather than reducing it. Waiting for a complaint or ignoring the chart based on feel defeats the entire purpose of monitoring the process.

Control charts and specification limits are separate concepts, and one being in order does not guarantee the other.

It applies to both variable and attribute data, not just attributes. Control limits are still calculated and used; they are not replaced by simple tolerance limits.

The lower control limit in this case is 9 minus 9, or 0, since it cannot go negative. Any value that does not add exactly three times the square root of the average count to itself does not match the standard c chart formula.

These rules apply to variables charts as well as attribute charts. Counting every point within the run as its own violation overstates the number of distinct signals present.

Cutting speed is the factor, and each distinct value tested is one level of that factor. A treatment refers to a specific combination of factor levels applied to an experimental unit, not a single factor's settings. Blocks and replicates describe experimental structure and repetition, not individual factor settings.

The correct answer is D (0.78): Cpk equals the minimum of (USL minus mean) divided by 3 sigma and (mean minus LSL) divided by 3 sigma, which is the minimum of 7 divided by 9 and 13 divided by 9, giving 0.78. This value is well below the process Cp of 1.11, which would apply if the process were perfectly centered. The gap between Cp and Cpk shows the process is shifted toward the upper specification limit rather than being limited by excessive spread. A Cpk this low signals the process cannot consistently meet both limits without recentering.
  150. The remaining subgroups cluster near the centerline with no visible trend, ruling out a gradual drift explanation.   151. The correct answer is A (720): PPM is calculated as the number of defective units divided by the total units inspected, multiplied by one million, which is 18 divided by 25,000 times 1,000,000, equaling 720. This metric expresses the defect rate on a standardized million-unit basis, allowing comparison across processes with different sample sizes. The other options result from common arithmetic errors such as doubling the count or misplacing a decimal in the division. PPM is a straightforward proportion scaled for readability, not a rate requiring additional adjustment.
  152. The correct answer is B (An estimate of pure experimental error through replication): Running the same treatment combination multiple times, with the setup independently reset each time, captures the natural run-to-run variation that exists even under identical conditions. This variation forms the basis for estimating experimental error, which is essential for testing whether observed factor effects are statistically significant. Randomization refers to the order in which different runs are performed, not to repeating a single treatment. Blocking and confounding address nuisance variables and aliasing structure, neither of which this repeated-run practice addresses.
  153. The correct answer is C (Natural process limits reflect the voice of the process, while specification limits reflect the voice of the customer): Natural limits, typically plus or minus three sigma from the process mean, describe what the process is actually capable of producing based on its inherent variation. Specification limits are set externally by customer or engineering requirements and describe what output is considered acceptable. These two sets of limits are calculated from entirely different sources and are not automatically linked or forced to match. A capable process is one where the natural limits fall comfortably inside the specification limits, but the two remain conceptually and numerically independent.
  154. The correct answer is D (The process produces units outside both specification limits, indicating inadequate capability): The histogram shows measurable counts in the 0-5 mL bin, which falls below the LSL of 5 mL, and in the 25-30 mL bin, which falls above the USL of 25 mL. Having nonconforming output on both tails simultaneously points to a spread problem rather than a simple centering issue. This pattern rules out the claim that the process spread fits comfortably within the specification limits. A histogram showing out-of-spec material on both sides indicates the process variation itself needs to be reduced, not just recentered.
  155. The correct answer is A (0.67): Cpm equals the tolerance width divided by six times the square root of sigma squared plus the squared deviation of the mean from target, which is 20 divided by six times the square root of 9 plus 16, or 20 divided by 30, equaling 0.67. Because the mean of 44 sits four units away from the target of 40, the Cpm calculation penalizes this off-target performance more heavily than Cp or Cpk would. This lower value compared to a simple Cp calculation reflects the added weight Cpm places on hitting the target, not just staying inside the limits. In this particular case Cpm and Cpk happen to come out equal because the numbers form a 3-4-5 relationship, but that is not a general rule; Cpm can be greater than, equal to, or less than Cpk depending on the specific numbers involved.
  156. The correct answer is D (Confounding (aliasing)): Confounding, also called aliasing, occurs when a fractional factorial design cannot separate the effect of one factor or interaction from another because they share the identical estimation pattern in the reduced design. This is an inherent tradeoff of using fewer runs than a full factorial requires. Blocking addresses known nuisance variables rather than the estimability of factor effects. Randomization and replication control for uncontrolled variation and error estimation, but neither one resolves an aliasing structure built into the design itself.
  157. The correct answer is B (4,000): DPMO is calculated as the total defects divided by the product of units and opportunities per unit, multiplied by one million, which is 80 divided by 500 times 40, or 80 divided by 20,000, times 1,000,000, equaling 4,000. Using opportunities per unit rather than just unit count accounts for the fact that a more complex product has more chances for a defect to occur. Failing to include the 40 opportunities per board in the denominator would produce an inflated and misleading defect rate. This standardized measure allows fair comparison of defect performance across products with very different complexity levels.
  158. The correct answer is C (Subgroup 6 exceeds the upper control limit, signaling a special cause): The fraction defective for subgroup 6 is 0.11, which is above the UCL of 0.10, making it a clear out-of-control signal on this p-chart. A single point beyond a control limit always warrants investigation into an assignable cause rather than being treated as ordinary variation. The remaining nine subgroups stay comfortably between the LCL and UCL, showing no other unusual pattern. Because the LCL on this chart is 0, no subgroup can violate the lower limit, so any special-cause signal here must come from the upper side.
  159. The correct answer is A (0.80): The capability ratio Cr is defined as the reciprocal of Cp, so Cr equals one divided by 1.25, which is 0.80. Cr expresses what proportion of the specification width is consumed by the natural process spread, so a smaller Cr indicates a more capable process. A Cr of 0.80 means the process spread uses 80 percent of the available tolerance, leaving some margin. Confusing Cr with Cp itself, or inverting the calculation incorrectly, produces the other listed distractor values.
  160. The correct answer is D (To average out the effects of uncontrolled or unknown nuisance variables over time): Randomizing run order spreads the influence of unknown or uncontrolled variables, such as gradual equipment warm-up or ambient condition drift, evenly across all treatment combinations. This prevents such nuisance effects from being mistakenly attributed to a specific factor being studied. Randomization does not reduce the number of runs needed, nor does it remove the requirement for statistical analysis afterward. It also does not by itself guarantee equal sample sizes, which is a separate design consideration handled through balanced allocation.
  161. The correct answer is C (3.5%): Percent defective is calculated as the number of defective units divided by the total lot size, multiplied by 100, which is 42 divided by 1,200 times 100, equaling 3.5 percent. This straightforward proportion converts a raw defect count into a standardized rate that can be compared across lots of different sizes. Misreading the numerator or denominator, or rounding incorrectly, produces the nearby distractor values. Percent defective is one of the most basic quality metrics and underlies more complex measures such as PPM and DPMO.
  162. The correct answer is B (0.89): The one-sided upper index is 28.0 minus 24.8 divided by three times 1.2, equaling 3.2 divided by 3.6, or 0.89, while the lower index is 24.8 minus 20.0 divided by 3.6, equaling 4.8 divided by 3.6, or 1.33. Cpk takes the minimum of these two one-sided values, so the correct Cpk is 0.89, not the larger lower-side figure. This shows the process mean sits closer to the upper specification limit, making the upper side the limiting factor for capability. Reporting the larger of the two one-sided indices instead of the smaller is a common calculation error that overstates true capability.
  163. The correct answer is A (Pp incorporates both common-cause and special-cause variation over time, so its estimate of spread is larger): Pp uses the overall standard deviation calculated from all the individual data points collected over an extended period, which naturally captures shifts, drifts, and special causes that occur between subgroups. Cp uses only the within-subgroup variation, which reflects short-term, common-cause variation under stable conditions. Because total long-term variation is almost always greater than or equal to short-term variation, Pp is typically lower than Cp for the same characteristic. This distinction is why Pp and Ppk are described as performance indices reflecting actual demonstrated output, rather than potential capability under ideal short-term conditions.
  164. The correct answer is C (8): A full factorial design with three factors, each at two levels, requires two raised to the third power runs, which equals 8. This count represents every possible combination of the low and high settings across all three factors. Testing fewer combinations, such as 6, would leave some factor-level combinations unexplored and unable to be estimated. Testing 16 runs would actually correspond to four factors at two levels, not three, so it overstates the requirement for this design.
  165. The correct answer is D (Blocking on supplier lot): Blocking is the design principle specifically intended to control for a known nuisance variable, such as material lot, by grouping runs so that lot-to-lot differences do not get mistaken for true factor effects. Running all treatments within each block, using material from a single lot, isolates the lot variation from the factor effects being studied. Randomization alone helps with unknown nuisance sources but does not remove the systematic effect of a known variable like lot identity. Confounding a known nuisance with an interaction would actually make the interaction effect uninterpretable, which is the opposite of the intended control.
  166. The correct answer is B (Observation 6 falls below the lower control limit, indicating a special cause): The value of 85 at observation 6 is below the LCL of 88, which is a clear violation of the lower control limit on this individuals chart. A point outside either control limit always signals a special cause requiring investigation, regardless of whether it falls above or below the centerline. The surrounding observations all sit comfortably between 88 and 112, showing no other unusual pattern in the data. This single low reading being centered exactly on target is incorrect, since the chart shows a clear departure from the established process behavior.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Six Sigma): A defect rate of 3.4 parts per million is the benchmark commonly associated with Six Sigma quality performance, representing a very low long-term defect rate after accounting for a typical 1.5 sigma process shift. Lower sigma levels, such as Three Sigma or Four Sigma, correspond to substantially higher defect rates measured in the tens of thousands or hundreds of thousands per million. This benchmark is widely used in quality engineering to communicate process performance using a single, easily compared number. The 3.4 PPM figure is specifically tied to the Six Sigma level, not any of the lower sigma tiers.
  2. The correct answer is D (Cpk would be zero or negative): When the process mean falls outside a specification limit, the distance from the mean to that limit becomes zero or negative, and since Cpk uses the smaller of the two one-sided calculations, the overall Cpk value becomes zero or negative as well. This mathematically confirms that the process is producing output beyond the specification limit for at least part of the distribution. Cpk remains fully calculable in this situation, it simply produces a value that signals severe incapability rather than becoming undefined. A negative or zero Cpk is a strong, unambiguous indicator that immediate corrective action is needed.
  3. The correct answer is B (The response variable): The response variable is the measured outcome of interest in a designed experiment, and in this case that outcome is cookie diameter. Oven temperature and bake time are the factors being manipulated, not the outcome being measured. A treatment level refers to a specific setting of one factor, and a block refers to a grouping variable used to control nuisance variation, neither of which describes the measured output itself. Clearly identifying the response variable before running an experiment ensures the correct data is collected to evaluate the factor effects.
  4. The correct answer is C (The process spread is well within both specification limits, suggesting good capability): The histogram data ranges from 34 mm to 49 mm, comfortably inside the USL of 50 mm and LSL of 30 mm, with no counts appearing near or beyond either limit. A spread that occupies only a portion of the available tolerance, centered well away from both limits, is consistent with a high capability index. There is no indication of a bimodal shape, since the counts rise smoothly to a single peak and then decline. Claiming the spread nearly equals the tolerance width is contradicted by the clear margin visible between the data range and both specification limits.
  5. The correct answer is A (Cpk is always less than or equal to Cp): Cpk accounts for both the process spread and how well the process is centered between the specification limits, while Cp only measures spread relative to tolerance width assuming perfect centering. Because centering can only reduce or maintain the effective capability, never improve it beyond the potential capability, Cpk can never exceed Cp. The two indices become equal only in the special case where the process is perfectly centered between the limits. This relationship makes the gap between Cp and Cpk a useful diagnostic for identifying centering problems separately from spread problems.
  6. The correct answer is D (An interaction effect): An interaction effect exists when the influence of one factor on the response depends on the level of another factor, meaning the factors do not act independently. This is distinct from a main effect, which describes the average influence of a single factor across all levels of the other factors. Confounding refers to the inability to separate two effects statistically within a design, which is a different concept from a genuine interaction between factors. Recognizing interactions is a key reason factorial designs are preferred over changing one factor at a time, since one-factor-at-a-time approaches cannot detect this kind of dependency.
  7. The correct answer is C (3,000): DPMO is calculated as total defects divided by the product of units inspected and opportunities per unit, multiplied by one million, which is 36 divided by 2,000 times 6, or 36 divided by 12,000, times 1,000,000, equaling 3,000. Including all 6 weld joints per unit as separate opportunities correctly scales the defect rate to reflect the true complexity of the inspected item. Omitting the opportunities per unit factor, or using it incorrectly, produces the inflated or deflated distractor values shown. This calculation allows welding defect performance to be benchmarked consistently against other processes with different numbers of inspection points.
  8. The correct answer is A (Subgroup 6 exceeds the upper control limit, indicating a special cause requiring investigation): The count of 12 nonconforming units at subgroup 6 is above the UCL of 11, which is a direct violation of the upper control limit on this np-chart. Any point beyond a control limit represents a statistically significant departure from expected common-cause variation and must be investigated. The remaining subgroups all fall between 3 and 6 nonconforming units, well within the established control limits. A shift toward improvement would show a sustained downward trend rather than a single dramatic spike above the upper limit.
  9. The correct answer is B (It allows estimation of experimental error and increases the precision of effect estimates): Repeating each treatment combination multiple times generates the variability data needed to calculate a valid estimate of experimental error, which is essential for testing whether observed effects are statistically significant rather than random noise. This repetition also narrows the confidence interval around each estimated effect, improving the overall precision of the experiment's conclusions. Randomization of run order is a separate design step addressing a different source of bias, unrelated to how many times each treatment is repeated. Replication does not automatically resolve confounding or reduce the number of factors under study, since those are determined by the overall design structure, not by repeat counts.
  10. The correct answer is D (1.33): Cp is calculated as the tolerance width divided by six times sigma, which is 510 minus 490 divided by six times 2.5, equaling 20 divided by 15, or 1.33. This value reflects the potential capability of the process assuming it is perfectly centered between the specification limits. A Cp of 1.33 is generally considered an acceptable minimum benchmark in many industries, though actual centering must still be checked using Cpk. Errors in dividing the tolerance width or miscounting the sigma multiplier produce the other listed distractor values.
  11. The correct answer is C (8): A half-fraction of a 2^4 design reduces the full 16-run factorial to half that number, which is 8 runs. This reduction is achieved by selecting a defining relation that confounds certain higher-order interactions with the remaining effects, trading some estimability for a substantial reduction in experimental effort. The full 2^4 factorial itself would require 16 runs, which is twice what the half-fraction design needs. Choosing 4 or 12 runs would not correspond to a proper half-fraction of this particular full factorial structure.
  12. The correct answer is B (A full 2^2 factorial design): The table lists all four possible combinations of the two coded levels, -1 and +1, across two factors, which is exactly the full set of combinations required for a 2^2 full factorial design. A one-factor-at-a-time design would only vary one factor while holding the other constant, which does not match the table showing both factors varying together. A half-fraction design of this two-factor structure would use fewer than four runs, and a randomized block design would require an additional blocking column not present here. Seeing all four combinations of two two-level factors is the defining signature of a complete 2^2 factorial matrix.

Reporting the larger lower-side value instead of the minimum is a frequent calculation mistake that overstates the true capability.   14. The correct answer is D (A main effect): A main effect measures the average change in the response caused by moving a single factor from its low level to its high level, averaged over all combinations of the other factors in the experiment. This is distinct from an interaction effect, which specifically captures how one factor's influence changes depending on another factor's level. Confounded effects refer to a design limitation where two effects cannot be separated, and block effects capture variation due to a grouping variable rather than the factors under study. Main effects are typically the first quantities examined when analyzing factorial experiment results.

This conversion shows how small a PPM figure actually is when expressed as a more familiar percentage, since 250 PPM represents a very low defect rate. Misplacing the decimal point by one or two positions produces the larger distractor values shown. Understanding this conversion helps quality engineers communicate defect performance clearly to audiences more familiar with percentages than PPM.

A point beyond either control limit is never treated as ordinary common-cause variation and must be investigated for an assignable cause such as a material or process change.   17. The correct answer is B (Experimental error (residual)): Experimental error, also called the residual, captures the variation in the response that remains unexplained after accounting for the treatment effects, block effects, and interactions included in the model. This unexplained variation arises from natural process noise, measurement variation, and any factors not included in the experimental design. The treatment effect and main effect describe the systematic influence of the factors being studied, which is the opposite of unexplained variation. Block effects specifically capture variation attributable to a known grouping variable, not the leftover, unaccounted-for portion of the data.

A Cpk value alone never guarantees a specific defect count such as zero, since it is a statistical index based on assumed distribution shape, not a direct defect tally. The correct answer is A (It reduces the required runs from 32 to 16 by confounding higher-order interactions with main effects): A full 2^5 factorial requires 32 runs, and the half-fraction 2^(5-1) design cuts this to 16 runs by using the defining relation I=ABCDE to alias certain interactions with other effects. This tradeoff sacrifices the ability to independently estimate every possible interaction in exchange for a substantial reduction in experimental cost and time. The design does not double the run count, which would move in the opposite direction of the intended efficiency gain. Fractional designs still require careful randomization and, where practical, replication to obtain valid error estimates, so neither of those practices is eliminated by using a fraction.

  21. The correct answer is C (Processes where hitting a target value matters as much as staying within limits): Cpm specifically penalizes deviation from a stated target value in its denominator, making it sensitive to off-target performance even when a unit technically falls within the specification limits. This makes Cpm especially valuable for characteristics where consistently hitting a nominal value, not just avoiding out-of-spec output, is critical to product performance. Cp and Cpk do not include this target-deviation penalty, so they cannot distinguish a well-centered process from one that is merely inside the limits but consistently off-target. Cpm is designed for continuous, target-driven data and is not applicable to simple pass/fail attribute inspection results.
  22. The correct answer is D (A randomized complete block design, blocking on operator): Blocking on operator groups the experimental runs by a known source of variation, the individual operator, while randomizing the order of the four treatments within each operator's session to control for uncontrolled nuisance effects. This structure isolates operator-to-operator differences from the true treatment effects being compared. A completely randomized design with no blocking would ignore the operator grouping entirely, which does not match the described session structure. This scenario also does not match a full factorial design, since operator is being used as a blocking variable rather than as a crossed experimental factor with its own levels of interest.

This makes a consistent, well-documented opportunity definition essential for tracking genuine process improvement over time or comparing performance across different products. DPMO does require knowing both defects and opportunities, but the total number of units alone is not sufficient without also fixing the opportunity definition. Opportunities are central to the DPMO calculation itself, not a factor limited to Cpk or other unrelated metrics.

This relationship means a smaller Cr always corresponds to a larger Cp, since a smaller fraction of the tolerance being consumed by the process spread indicates greater capability. Simply restating the given Cr value as the answer, or squaring it, produces the incorrect distractor values shown. Understanding this reciprocal relationship allows quality engineers to convert freely between the two related capability metrics as needed.
  26. The correct answer is D (It cannot detect interaction effects between temperature and pressure): Testing one factor at a time while holding the other constant can identify each factor's individual main effect, but it structurally cannot reveal whether the two factors interact, since the combinations needed to observe an interaction are never actually run together at varied levels. A factorial design, by contrast, tests all combinations and can estimate interactions directly from the resulting data. This one-factor-at-a-time approach can sometimes require fewer or a similar number of runs depending on the number of levels tested, so run count alone is not the defining limitation. Randomization can still be applied to a one-factor-at-a-time approach, so the inability to randomize is not the correct limitation either.
  27. The correct answer is A (3.5%): Percent defective is the number of defective units divided by the sample size, multiplied by 100, which is 28 divided by 800 times 100, equaling 3.5 percent. This calculation converts the raw defect count into a standardized rate comparable across samples of different sizes. This basic percent defective calculation underlies many of the more advanced quality metrics used to track and report process performance.
  28. The correct answer is B (Ppk = 0.95): Ppk is a performance index calculated using the overall, long-term standard deviation, which inherently captures both common-cause and special-cause variation that occurred during the data collection period. Cp and Cpk, by contrast, use the within-subgroup standard deviation, which reflects only short-term, common-cause variation under presumably stable conditions. Because Ppk reflects everything that actually happened over the long term, it is the appropriate index for describing demonstrated real-world performance rather than potential capability. The four values shown are clearly not identical, since Cp, Cpk, Pp, and Ppk each use different combinations of centering and variation estimates.
  29. The correct answer is C (An observed main effect might actually be caused by an aliased two-factor interaction, complicating interpretation): In a Resolution III design, main effects and two-factor interactions share the same estimation pattern, meaning a statistically significant result attributed to a main effect could actually be driven by an aliased interaction instead. This ambiguity requires careful judgment, follow-up experimentation, or a higher-resolution design when interactions are suspected to be important. Randomization remains necessary in a Resolution III design just as in any other experimental design, since aliasing addresses estimability, not noise control. Two-factor interactions are not impossible to estimate in general, they are simply confounded with main effects in this specific low-resolution structure.
  30. The correct answer is D (Cpk will equal Cp): When a process is perfectly centered between its specification limits, the distance from the mean to the upper limit equals the distance from the mean to the lower limit, making the two one-sided capability calculations identical. Since Cpk takes the minimum of two equal values, it produces the same result as Cp under this specific centering condition. This is the only situation in which the two indices coincide exactly, since any departure from perfect centering makes one one-sided value smaller than the other. Cpk remains fully calculable when a process is centered, so there is no scenario in which centering prevents the calculation.
  31. The correct answer is A (A treatment (treatment combination)): A treatment, or treatment combination, refers to the specific pairing of one level of factor A with one level of factor B that is actually applied to an experimental unit during a run. A replicate refers to repeating an entire treatment combination multiple times, which is a distinct concept from the combination itself. A block is a grouping variable used to control nuisance variation, and a response is the measured outcome resulting from applying the treatment, neither of which describes the factor-level combination itself. Understanding the treatment concept is fundamental to correctly setting up and interpreting any factorial experiment.

  33. The correct answer is D (Blocking groups experimental units to control a known nuisance variable, while randomization protects against unknown or uncontrolled variables): Blocking is used when a specific nuisance source, such as operator, machine, or material lot, is known in advance and can be deliberately grouped to remove its effect from the factor comparisons. Randomization instead protects against variables that are unknown, unmeasured, or simply impractical to control directly, by spreading their influence evenly across all treatments. Neither principle inherently increases the total number of experimental runs required, since both can often be implemented within the same overall run count. Randomization is a general practice applied across full factorial, fractional factorial, and other design types, not a technique reserved exclusively for fractional designs.

Detection reflects how effective existing controls are at identifying the failure before it escapes to the next process step or the customer. Keeping these two ratings conceptually separate is essential because they respond to different types of corrective action.

Both scopes are necessary and complementary but are evaluated using different stakeholders, data, and criteria.

  64. The correct answer is C (The zone combining high severity with high occurrence, generally shown in red.): The zone combining high severity with high occurrence, generally shown in red. In a typical risk matrix, the zone where high severity intersects with high occurrence represents the greatest combined risk and is usually flagged in red to signal urgent mitigation. Low severity and low occurrence combinations generally fall into a green zone requiring little or no immediate action. Color coding is specifically intended to guide prioritization, so it does carry meaningful weight in decision making.
  65. The correct answer is D (Risk 2, because a repeated safety interlock failure poses a high severity hazard requiring urgent attention.): Risk 2, because a repeated safety interlock failure poses a high severity hazard requiring urgent attention. Risk 2 describes a critical safety interlock that has already failed twice in a short period, indicating a high severity hazard with demonstrated occurrence that warrants prompt escalation to senior management. Risk 1 describes only a minor, non-functional cosmetic defect that does not carry the same safety implications. Treating both risks identically or ignoring them both fails to reflect the significant difference in severity and urgency described in the excerpt.
  66. The correct answer is B (Thinking ahead about what could go wrong before it happens and planning to prevent or catch it.): Thinking ahead about what could go wrong before it happens and planning to prevent or catch it. Risk-based thinking, described in plain language, means anticipating potential problems before they occur and building in ways to prevent or catch them early. This proactive mindset applies across everyday operator decisions as well as formal engineering assessments. Waiting for a customer to find a defect or assuming a process is already perfect runs counter to this proactive philosophy.
  67. The correct answer is A (An identical score can mask very different risk profiles and should not be the sole prioritization basis.): An identical score can mask very different risk profiles and should not be the sole prioritization basis. Two failure modes can reach the same risk priority number through very different combinations of severity, occurrence, and detection, meaning the score alone does not reveal the nature of the underlying risk. This is why teams are cautioned against using the risk priority number as the only prioritization tool, especially when severity differs. A shared score does not indicate a shared root cause, and equal scores are entirely possible in practice.
  68. Assigning clear ownership for each identified risk ensures that someone is responsible for tracking and completing the necessary mitigation actions. Without a named owner, risks can languish unresolved because no one feels directly accountable for closing them out. This assignment does not legally transfer risk away from the organization or restrict awareness to a single department.

Relying entirely on one aging piece of equipment with no backup creates a single point of failure, meaning an unexpected breakdown could stop the critical process step and halt production. This is a classic operational risk tied to process capability and continuity rather than a financial, marketing, or long-term strategic concern. Identifying this exposure typically prompts consideration of backup equipment or preventive maintenance improvements.

When two departments apply different thresholds to the same type of hazard, the organization risks inconsistent decision making and unclear accountability for similar risks. Aligning both departments to one documented, appropriate threshold restores consistency and fairness in how risk is judged. Simply picking the higher number or abandoning criteria altogether does not resolve the underlying inconsistency in a defensible way.

The risk priority number is the product of severity, occurrence, and detection, so with severity at 6, the new occurrence at 2, and detection unchanged at 4, the calculation is 6 times 2 times 4, which equals 48. This recalculation shows a meaningful reduction from the original 120 achieved solely by lowering occurrence through the design change. The number does not remain unchanged, nor does reducing occurrence cause severity to rise.

Process improvements that reduce occurrence or improve detection do not change what would happen if the failure still occurred. Option A is wrong because operator training relates to occurrence and skill, not the inherent severity of a failure's effect. Reputational risk spans the whole organization and its relationship with customers broadly, making it a natural fit for enterprise level categorization rather than a single facility or part number. The other options describe localized, specific issues tied to one machine, one gauge, or one part at one site, which are better classified as operational or product level risks. Enterprise categories are meant to capture exposures that affect the organization as a whole.
  74. The correct answer is B (Reducing the likelihood of occurrence or eliminating the cause is generally preferred over improving detection alone.): Reducing the likelihood of occurrence or eliminating the cause is generally preferred over improving detection alone. Sound risk reduction practice favors addressing the root cause to lower occurrence, since this prevents the failure from happening rather than only improving the chance of catching it afterward. Detection improvements are still valuable but are generally considered a secondary layer of protection rather than the first choice. Severity is rarely the easiest or most appropriate target since it usually requires a fundamental design change to shift.

Risk appetite describes the level of risk an organization is willing to tolerate while still pursuing its strategic and operational objectives. This concept helps guide decisions about which risks require mitigation and which fall within an acceptable range. It is a broader strategic concept rather than a specific budget figure, complaint count, or facility measurement.

Leaving the process unchanged or removing the detection column ignores a clear risk indicator identified through the FMEA. An escalation path defines how and to whom a risk that exceeds documented acceptability criteria should be reported, ensuring timely action from people with the authority to resolve it. Without this defined path, high risk issues could remain unresolved at a level unable to act on them. It does not guarantee automatic resolution or remove other documentation requirements within the plan.
  78. The correct answer is B (Risk identification recognizes what could go wrong, while risk assessment evaluates likelihood and impact.): Risk identification recognizes what could go wrong, while risk assessment evaluates likelihood and impact. Risk identification is the step of recognizing potential hazards or failure modes that could occur, while risk assessment follows by evaluating how likely each identified risk is and how severe its impact would be. These are sequential but distinct steps within a broader risk management process. Treating them as identical or reversing their order undermines the logical flow of a proper risk assessment.
  79. Even after a risk priority number falls below the acceptability threshold, conditions like process drift, equipment wear, or control degradation can change occurrence or detection over time, potentially raising the true risk again. Periodic review protects against this drift and ensures the residual risk assessment remains valid. Assuming permanent compliance or prohibiting any further monitoring ignores this real possibility of change.
  80. The correct answer is A (A structured, documented method for identifying failure modes, their effects, and prioritizing mitigation efforts.): A structured, documented method for identifying failure modes, their effects, and prioritizing mitigation efforts. FMEA gives a team a consistent, documented framework for identifying potential failure modes, understanding their effects, and prioritizing which ones need mitigation based on calculated risk. This structured approach improves communication and traceability across the team and over time. It complements rather than replaces other quality tools like control plans and capability studies, and it does not guarantee failures will never occur.
  81. The correct answer is C (Severity of the potential harm and probability of occurrence): A risk matrix plots how bad an outcome could be against how likely that outcome is to happen. Plotting these two dimensions together lets a team visually separate risks that need immediate attention from those that are tolerable as is. Cost, detection difficulty, and cycle time may influence later decisions but they are not the defining axes of a risk matrix. This two-dimensional view is the foundation for setting priority zones such as high, medium, and low risk.

Multiplying rather than adding preserves the compounding effect of a failure that is both severe and hard to detect. Teams use this product to rank failure modes and decide where mitigation effort is spent first. The other formulas described do not match the standard FMEA calculation method.
  83. The correct answer is D (A predetermined acceptability threshold established before the analysis began): Setting the cutoff value in advance keeps the decision objective and prevents the team from adjusting the standard to fit whatever results the analysis produces. Any failure mode scoring above the threshold is flagged for mitigation, regardless of how it compares to other items on the same list. Comparing only against an average or against one extreme case can hide risks that individually deserve action. A fixed criterion applied consistently is what makes the acceptability decision defensible during an audit.
  84. This makes cross-functional discussion faster and reduces disagreement over minor point differences in the raw score. Qualitative labels do not remove the need for further assessment, a register, or ongoing mitigation work. They function alongside the numeric score rather than replacing it.
  85. This ordering protects the objectivity and repeatability of the risk decision, which matters greatly during an audit or regulatory review. Skipping documentation, letting a supplier set internal criteria, or forcing identical scores are not the reasons criteria are set early. The goal is consistency, not convenience.
  86. The correct answer is C (An identical RPN can hide a much higher severity rating): Because RPN is a single product of three factors, very different combinations can multiply to the same number, so a failure mode with catastrophic severity but low occurrence can score the same as a minor, frequent one. This is a well documented weakness of the method, which is why many organizations also review the severity rating on its own regardless of the combined score. The other statements describe behavior RPN does not actually have. Recognizing this limitation is why some teams add a severity-based override rule to their process.
  87. Placement there triggers an immediate response, typically avoidance or mitigation, rather than routine monitoring. Deferring it to an annual review, hiding it from customers, or reclassifying it as something else would defeat the purpose of the matrix. The visual zone exists precisely so this kind of risk cannot be overlooked.

A rating of nine indicates the current inspection or test methods have almost no chance of catching the problem before it escapes to the next stage or the customer. This is the opposite of confident detection, which would instead earn a low number near one. Occurrence and severity remain separate ratings and are never replaced by the detection score.
