Separate spreadsheets tend to drift out of sync and make it hard to spot risks that affect more than one function. The register does not eliminate the need for departmental assessment, guarantee a closure timeline, or substitute for a documented procedure. It is a tracking tool that supports the broader risk management process.
  91. The correct answer is B (Assigned owner, planned mitigation, current status, and residual risk level): These fields turn a simple list of risks into something that can actually be managed, since they show who is accountable, what action is planned, how far along it is, and what risk remains after treatment. Without them the register becomes a static list with no way to track progress or close items out. Product color, shift, complaint numbers, and cafeteria location are not core fields a risk register needs. The essential columns are the ones that support ownership and follow-through.
  92. The correct answer is C (The register should be updated whenever new information changes a risk): A risk register is meant to be a living document that reflects the current understanding of risk, so it needs updating whenever a design change, a new complaint, or a process change alters the likelihood or severity of an existing item. Leaving it frozen at the design phase means it no longer reflects reality and cannot support good decisions later in the product life cycle. Registers are not one-time deliverables, are not optional once approved, and are not restricted to a single viewer. Ongoing maintenance is what keeps the tool useful.
  93. The correct answer is A (To verify the documented process is genuinely followed in practice): An audit that only checks completed forms can miss a gap between what the procedure says and what people actually do on the floor. Interviewing staff and observing real activity confirms whether the risk management process is a living practice or just paperwork produced after the fact. Bonus decisions, reducing the logging requirement, and replacing forms with a checklist are not objectives of this kind of audit. The goal is to confirm genuine conformance, not just documented conformance.
  94. The correct answer is D (As a nonconformance because the register was not updated as required): A risk register that no longer reflects a known design change fails to meet the basic requirement that it be maintained as new information becomes available. This gap means new risks introduced by the change may never have been assessed at all, which is a genuine process failure rather than a trivial issue. It does not indicate a template problem or say anything about whether the design change itself was approved. The correct classification drives a corrective action to update the register and prevent recurrence.
  95. The correct answer is C (Verify objective evidence that the implemented action actually reduced the risk): Marking an action as closed in a register only shows that someone completed a task, not that the task actually reduced severity, occurrence, or improved detection. A thorough audit checks for objective evidence, such as updated test data or a recalculated RPN, before accepting that the risk has genuinely been reduced. Accepting the status at face value or simply removing the risk from tracking would miss this verification step entirely. Changing the rating scale midstream would also undermine the comparability of the assessment.
  96. The correct answer is B (Audit higher risk product lines more frequently than lower risk ones): Directing audit resources toward the product lines with the greatest potential for harm makes better use of limited time and catches problems where the consequences of missing them would be most serious. Auditing every line on an identical fixed date ignores real differences in risk exposure between products. Skipping lines with no complaints history or auditing only once over a product's entire life would leave significant gaps in oversight. A risk-based audit schedule keeps attention proportional to actual exposure.
  97. The correct answer is D (Avoid, because the hazard has been removed from the design entirely): Avoidance means changing the design or process so the hazard no longer exists at all, rather than reducing its likelihood or covering it with a control. Because the sharp edge is engineered out of the part, there is no residual hazard left for an assembler to encounter during insertion. This differs from mitigation, where the hazard would remain but its severity or likelihood would be reduced by an added control. Since nothing was passed to another party and no risk was knowingly kept, transfer and accept do not apply here.
  98. The correct answer is A (Avoid, because the hazardous material is never introduced into the process): Choosing not to use a hazardous material at all, rather than trying to control its effects, is the definition of avoidance as a risk treatment strategy. Because the adhesive is never used, the associated respiratory hazard simply does not exist in this process. Mitigation would apply only if the team kept the adhesive but added ventilation or protective equipment to reduce exposure. Since the material was never adopted in the first place, there is nothing to accept and nothing being passed to a supplier to manage.
  99. The correct answer is C (Avoid, because the risky process is eliminated by ending production): Ending the activity that creates an intolerable risk, in this case the entire product line, removes the hazard from the organization's operations altogether. This is a broader application of avoidance beyond a single design change, showing that avoidance can extend to a business decision when a risk cannot be brought down to an acceptable level any other way. Slowing production would only reduce exposure rather than remove it, which is mitigation, not avoidance. Simply notifying customers or hoping another company licenses the process later does nothing to treat the current risk.
  100. The correct answer is B (Mitigate, because an engineering control reduces the consequence of the failure): Mitigation keeps the underlying hazard in the design but adds a control that lowers either how likely a failure is or how bad its consequences would be. The thermal cutoff switch does not stop the motor from ever overheating, but it does reduce the resulting damage, which lowers the severity rating in a future FMEA update. This is different from avoidance, which would require removing the overheating hazard entirely from the design. Purchasing a component from a vendor does not by itself make this a transfer of risk, since the manufacturer still owns responsibility for the safety outcome.
  101. The correct answer is A (Detection, because the new inspection increases the chance of catching the defect): Adding an inspection step is a classic mitigation aimed squarely at the detection rating, since it increases the likelihood that a defect already present will be caught before it moves downstream. It does not change how severe the consequence would be if the fastener were missing, nor does it reduce how often the fastener actually goes missing in the first place. Those would require a different kind of change, such as a process improvement or an automated fastening tool. Improving detection lowers the overall risk priority number without touching severity or occurrence.
  102. The correct answer is D (Mitigate, because redundancy reduces the impact if the primary source fails): Adding a backup power source is a mitigation technique because it keeps the possibility of a primary failure in place but reduces the consequence of that failure by allowing continued operation. The device still requires electrical power, so avoidance does not apply, and the manufacturer still bears responsibility for the device's safe operation regardless of which supplier made the battery. Redundancy is a widely used mitigation approach in safety-critical design specifically because it lowers severity without eliminating the underlying possibility of a single component failing.
  103. The correct answer is A (Transfer, because the financial consequence is shifted to the insurer): Purchasing insurance does not change the likelihood or severity of the underlying failure at all, it simply shifts the financial burden of that consequence to a third party willing to accept it for a premium. The defect can still occur exactly as before, so this is not avoidance, and nothing about the design has changed, so it is not mitigation. Accept would describe keeping the consequence in-house without any risk-sharing arrangement, which is the opposite of what insurance does. Transfer is the correct label whenever a contractual or financial arrangement moves consequence to another party.
  104. The correct answer is C (Transfer, because the liability clause shifts financial consequence to the supplier): The defining feature of this agreement is the contractual clause that moves the financial consequence of a torque-related failure onto the supplier, which is the hallmark of a transfer strategy rather than a change to the underlying risk itself. The manufacturer's own retained incoming inspection is a separate, ongoing detection activity and does not by itself represent acceptance of the risk. Some inspection still occurs on both sides, so avoidance does not apply, and while the supplier's torque inspection may improve detection, the liability clause is what defines this specific arrangement as a transfer. Contract terms like this one are a common way risk is transferred in a supply chain.
  105. The correct answer is D (Transfer, because recall costs are contractually shifted to the supplier): A warranty clause that assigns financial responsibility for a specific failure to the party best positioned to control it is a direct example of risk transfer through contract. The clause does not itself reduce how often the component might fail, so mitigation is not the primary effect described here, and the manufacturer has not stopped using the supplier, so avoidance does not apply. Nothing in the scenario suggests the manufacturer is simply hoping the recall never happens without any arrangement in place, which rules out accept. Shifting the consequence contractually to another party is the essence of transfer.
  106. The correct answer is B (Accept the risk as is, since it already falls below the threshold): When a risk already scores below the organization's own predetermined acceptability threshold, and the cost of further reducing it clearly outweighs the benefit, formally accepting the residual risk is the appropriate and economically sound decision. Halting production or escalating to senior management would be a disproportionate response to a low severity, low occurrence issue that is already within acceptable bounds. Asking a customer to sign a waiver for a purely cosmetic issue already deemed acceptable is unnecessary and would not reflect standard practice. Accept is the correct treatment whenever a risk is already tolerable and further reduction is not justified.
  107. The correct answer is A (Formally document and obtain sign-off accepting the remaining residual risk): Because mitigation almost never reduces a risk to exactly zero, the remaining residual risk must be consciously and formally accepted by someone with the authority to do so before the item can be considered closed. This creates a documented record that a real decision was made rather than the risk simply being forgotten. Deleting the entry, assuming no further consideration is needed, or forcing the severity rating artificially to zero would all obscure the fact that some risk genuinely remains. Formal acceptance is the final, documented step in the risk treatment process for any risk that cannot be reduced further.
  108. The correct answer is C (A refinement of the accept strategy to prepare for the risk): Accepting a risk does not mean ignoring it entirely, and pairing acceptance with a documented contingency plan is a recognized way to prepare a practical response in case the accepted risk materializes. This does not shift financial responsibility to the supplier, so it is not a transfer, and it does not lower the probability of the disruption happening in the first place, so it is not mitigation either. Nothing about having a contingency plan implies the original decision to accept rather than avoid was wrong. A well-prepared acceptance decision often includes exactly this kind of readiness planning.
  109. The correct answer is D (To capture and categorize field issues so patterns can be identified): A structured complaint tracking system exists so that individual field issues are recorded consistently enough to later be analyzed together, revealing patterns that a single complaint viewed in isolation would never show. This structured data becomes an important input into the ongoing risk monitoring process. Responding quickly to individual customers is a customer service goal that sits alongside this system rather than being its primary quality purpose, and the system does not limit how many complaints can be filed. It also does not replace a risk register, since the two serve different but connected functions.
  110. The correct answer is A (Trending analysis, which looks at complaint data over time as a group): Trending analysis looks at complaint or defect data collected across a period of time rather than judging each incident on its own, which is exactly how a slow, steady increase in a specific failure mode becomes visible. Looking only at the very first complaint, a single FMEA detection rating, or one incoming shipment would never reveal a pattern building gradually across months. This kind of analysis is a core input into ongoing risk monitoring, since it can surface a risk that individual complaint handling would miss entirely. Once identified, a trend like this typically triggers a reassessment of the associated risk.
  111. The correct answer is B (Initiate a corrective action and reassess the associated risk promptly): A confirmed trend that significantly exceeds normal variation is a clear signal that the current level of risk control is no longer adequate, and it should prompt a corrective action along with an updated risk assessment reflecting the new occurrence data. Waiting for a scheduled annual review would allow the underlying problem to continue affecting customers in the meantime. Closing the register entry ignores the trend entirely, and lowering the detection rating without any actual investigation would misrepresent the data rather than respond to it. Prompt action is what connects complaint trending to the rest of the risk management process.
  112. The correct answer is D (Post-market surveillance, which monitors the product throughout its market life): Post-market surveillance is the ongoing collection and analysis of real-world performance data for as long as a product remains available to customers, which is fundamentally different from a one-time activity performed before release. Design verification and process validation are both completed early in a product's life and are not repeated continuously afterward in the same way. Incoming inspection applies only to material entering the plant and has no connection to how a shipped product performs in the field. Post-market surveillance closes the loop between design assumptions and actual field experience.
  113. The correct answer is C (Update the risk file to include the newly discovered failure mode and assess it using the same acceptability criteria applied originally.): post-market surveillance is specifically intended to surface exactly this kind of gap, and any newly discovered failure mode must be added to the existing risk documentation and assessed using the same acceptability criteria applied during the original analysis. Ignoring it or waiting for a future redesign would leave a known hazard unaddressed for an indefinite period. Deleting the original assessment would also be wrong, since the prior analysis remains a valid historical record even as it is updated. Feeding field data back into the risk file is the core purpose of maintaining surveillance after release.
  114. The correct answer is A (Field failure reports, service records, and customer complaints combined): A robust post-market surveillance program draws on multiple real-world data streams together, since each source captures a different slice of actual field experience and no single source tells the whole story on its own. Relying only on the original FMEA, incoming inspection of one lot, or initial validation testing would provide no visibility at all into how the product behaves once it is actually in customer hands. Combining these live data sources is what allows emerging risks to be detected early enough to act on them. This combined view is a defining feature of an effective surveillance program.
  115. The correct answer is B (Risk monitoring must continue throughout the product's entire life cycle): Risk management is not a single event completed at one point in the design process, it is an ongoing activity that continues through production, distribution, and field use as new information becomes available. Treating the signed FMEA as the final word ignores the reality that occurrence rates, new failure modes, and changing use conditions can only be observed after launch. There is no rule that makes a completed FMEA invalid on a fixed timeline, and the manufacturer, not the customer, retains responsibility for ongoing monitoring of its own product. Continuous monitoring is what distinguishes a mature risk management system from a one-time compliance exercise.
  116. The correct answer is A (Avoid, by switching to the validated non-hazardous cleaning method): Since an equally effective, non-hazardous substitute already exists at a similar cost, switching to it removes the severe health hazard from the process entirely rather than merely reducing exposure to it, which is the definition of avoidance. Continuing to use the solvent because it has been tolerated historically ignores the fact that a better validated alternative is available at no real penalty. Asking operators to sign an acknowledgment form does not transfer any real consequence and offers no actual protection, and adding gloves would only mitigate exposure to a hazard that could instead be eliminated outright. When a genuinely equivalent safer alternative exists, avoidance is generally the preferred strategy over mitigation.
  117. Without these elements, a plan is just a description of an idea with no mechanism to ensure it is actually implemented or checked. Employee lists, purchase order numbers, and summaries of unrelated closed risks add no value to tracking this specific mitigation. These three elements are what allow a plan to be followed up on and closed with confidence.
  118. Nothing in the scenario indicates the risk level itself changed, so it cannot be assumed it dropped below the threshold on its own. The lack of progress is not evidence of a flawed template or an incorrect original rating, it is a direct consequence of missing accountability elements. Assigning an owner and due date is what turns a plan into real action.
  119. The correct answer is B (Collect new occurrence data and recalculate the RPN to confirm improvement): Verifying a mitigation actually works requires collecting fresh data after the change and recalculating the risk score to confirm the intended improvement genuinely occurred, rather than assuming installation alone guarantees the desired result. Relying on an unverified opinion or simply trusting the schedule was met does not provide objective evidence of a reduced risk. Closing the item immediately upon implementation skips the verification step entirely and could leave an ineffective control in place undetected. Objective data collected after the change is what confirms a mitigation earned its intended effect.
  120. The correct answer is D (A significant design change or an unusual spike in complaints): Events that genuinely change the underlying likelihood or severity of a risk, such as a design change or a sudden increase in complaints, are exactly the kind of trigger that should force an unscheduled reassessment rather than waiting for the next periodic review. A routine satisfaction survey, an on-time calibration, or a marketing brochure from a vendor have no bearing on the actual risk profile of the product. Building these genuine triggers into the procedure keeps new information from sitting unreviewed until the next calendar date arrives. This kind of event-driven reassessment is a key feature of an effective ongoing monitoring process.
  121. The correct answer is C (A documented risk-benefit analysis with authorized sign-off for the exception): When a residual risk remains above the normal acceptability threshold, formally accepting it requires a documented analysis weighing the risk against the clinical benefit, along with sign-off from someone authorized to approve that exception to the standard criteria. Assuming benefit alone automatically justifies any level of risk without documentation would bypass a required control step. Artificially rounding a rating down to zero misrepresents the actual analysis rather than resolving it, and an informal verbal agreement provides no defensible record for a regulatory review. A documented, authorized risk-benefit analysis is the standard mechanism for this kind of exception.
  122. The correct answer is B (The residual RPN, reflecting the risk remaining after mitigation was applied): Once a mitigation action has been implemented and new data collected, recalculating the risk priority number produces the residual RPN, which represents whatever risk still remains after the treatment rather than the original pre-mitigation value. The original two hundred forty figure is the baseline score, not the recalculated one, so labeling the new value as baseline would be backwards. There is no separate rating called a severity index or a fixed detection ceiling in standard FMEA terminology. Tracking the residual RPN against the original baseline is exactly how a team demonstrates that a mitigation genuinely reduced risk.
  123. The correct answer is A (Risk management informs and connects to design, production, and CAPA processes): Risk management activities generate information that directly feeds design decisions, production controls, supplier management, and corrective action processes, so keeping it integrated with the quality management system ensures that information actually reaches the people who need it to make decisions. Treating it as a disconnected standalone program would isolate valuable risk data from the very processes it is meant to influence. Regulation does not require risk activities to be run as separate standalone programs, and integration does not remove the need for dedicated tools such as an FMEA or a risk register. A connected system is what allows risk information to drive real improvement across the organization.
